->**BASIL ON <unclear>ANTIMONY</unclear>. 1678.**<-
<note>Original: "Anhelony." The text is damaged by cracks in the leather. Given the date and the author "Basil" (referring to the alchemist Basilius Valentinus), this word is almost certainly "Antimony," referencing his famous work *The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony*.</note>

<vocab>Basil, Antimony, 1678</vocab>

<center>
[Spine of the Book]
</center>

<note>This spine identifies the work of Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century figure. While once thought to be a monk, modern historians generally view "Basil Valentine" as a pseudonym used by various authors of alchemical texts. The year 1678 marks a significant English publication of his research into the chemical properties of the metal antimony.</note>

<summary>The spine of a 1678 leather-bound book titled "Basil on Antimony," likely the work of the famous alchemist Basilius Valentinus.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page is a back-matter advertisement for other books sold by the same publisher, Dorman Newman, who published the main text of "The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony." It provides a snapshot of the scientific and medical literature available in London in 1678.</meta>

->*These five books lately published are to be sold*<-
->*by* **Dorman Newman** *at the King’s Arms*<-
->*in the Poultry.*<- <note>The Poultry was a street in the City of London famous for its booksellers and markets.</note>

*Philosophical Dialogues*, concerning the principles of natural bodies, wherein the principles of the old and new philosophy are stated and the new demonstrated to be more agreeable to reason, based on mechanical experience and its usefulness for the benefit of mankind; in *duodecimo* <note>original: "in duodecimo"; a book size where each printed sheet is folded into 12 leaves, resulting in a small handheld volume.</note>.

A short essay regarding the history and cure of fevers, humbly proposed for the consideration of the Royal Society and the College of Physicians in order to improve medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> and thereby benefit our countrymen.

*Anatomical exercises in various regions of the human body, showing the structure and use of the parts, published for the use of modern doctors, surgeons, and pharmacists.* <note>original: "Exercitationes anatomicæ in varias regiones humani corporis, partium structuram atque usum ostendentes recentium Medicorum, Chirurgorum, nec non Pharmacopolarum in usum divulgatæ."</note>

*By Robert Bayfield, physician; in duodecimo.* <note>original: "A Roberto Bayfield medico."</note>

A treatise on gout, originally written in the French language by **Theodore Turquet de Mayerne**, Knight, chief physician to the late King and Queen of England. Translated into English <note>original: "Englished"</note> by **Thomas Sherley**, M.D., physician-in-ordinary to his present Majesty Charles II, with advice regarding hypochondriacal fits <note>In the 17th century, "hypochondriacal fits" referred to physical and emotional ailments believed to originate in the organs of the upper abdomen.</note>; to which is added medicinal counsels or advice by the same author; in *octavo* <note>original: "in octavo"; a book size where each sheet is folded into 8 leaves.</note>.

*The Remedy of Physicians* <note>original: "Medela Medicorum"</note>, showing the reasons and grounds for the contempt shown toward medicine and physicians; in *duodecimo*.

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface "To the Reader" for "The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony" by Basilius Valentinus. It follows a list of books sold by the publisher, Dorman Newman. The preface addresses the controversy surrounding antimony, which was often dismissed as a poison by theoretical scholars who lacked practical laboratory experience.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

<margin>m/</margin>

It is well known that the current subject, which this eminent author discusses so elegantly, was until recently generally (though most unfairly) branded with the detestable name of poison <note>original: "Poyson"</note>. This hatred <note>original: "Odium"</note> was cast upon it (as is shown in this book) in the days of the author; and perhaps, long before that time, it may have been condemned through the malice of idle theorists <note>original: "Speculators"</note>. 

For those men, who presume to be masters of all knowledge because they are book-learned <term>original: "Letter-learned"; possessing theoretical knowledge from reading without practical experience</term> in the theory of this or that art, without the slightest skill or will to exercise themselves in the actual practice <note>original: "Practice"</note> of it, must necessarily

<summary>This preface introduces the controversial history of antimony, defending it against the "poison" label given to it by theoretical scholars who lack practical medicinal experience.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, Basilius Valentinus, medical theory, medicinal practice, poison, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader," following the previous page's discussion on theoretical scholars who lack practical laboratory experience. It completes the thought that such men condemn what they do not understand to hide their own ignorance.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

condemn what they do not understand. And they are led to do this, lest by approving what they have no knowledge of, they should too clearly reveal their ignorance; because they are unable to demonstrate the reasons for their own approval. Therefore, it is highly probable that they—hating labor and envying the diligent <note>original: "sedulous"</note> efforts of others, for fear that their own reputation <note>original: "seeming Honour"</note> should be overshadowed—would rather (according to the old proverb <note>The author refers to the proverb "Give a dog an ill name and hang him," suggesting that once something is labeled "poison," it is unfairly rejected without trial.</note>) give *antimony* <note>original: "Antimony"</note> a bad name than dirty their hands by testing the medicinal properties <term>original: "Virtues"; the inherent power or efficacy of a medicinal substance</term> with which it is endowed. 

How poorly it has been spoken of in our time is well known to many; and how useful it has been in the cure of many terrible <note>original: "deplorable"</note> diseases within the last twenty years is just as well understood by most of the hard-working <note>original: "industriously laborious"</note> physicians of this city <note>Likely referring to London, where the publisher was based.</note>, who can, and daily do,

<vocab>Antimony, Physicians, Diseases, Properties, Proverb</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that critics condemn antimony out of laziness and a fear of exposing their own ignorance, while practical physicians have proven its medicinal value through twenty years of successful use.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, medical practice, physician, medicinal properties, medical theory, London</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader," following the discussion of practical physicians who testify to the medicinal power of antimony based on their successful treatments.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

Whenever they meet with men of similar industry, they testify for the author that a **TRIUMPHANT CHARIOT** is not undeservedly assigned to **ANTIMONY**. For in ancient Rome, no one was permitted to enter in a triumphant chariot who had not slain at least five thousand *enemies* and obtained a complete victory. 

Therefore, if *antimony* (through the gift of the *Most* High implanted <note>original: "insited"</note> within it) is more capable than any other single substance <term>original: "Simple"; a medicinal mineral or plant used on its own rather than as part of a compound</term> in nature of subduing and expelling countless diseases—the enemies of human life—as it is certainly known to do, and of obtaining a total victory over them, why should any man resent that by which his own or his neighbor’s life is, or may be, so well preserved? 

It is true that if taken without preparation, or if it is poorly prepared (because of the significant *evil* mixed with its great *good*), it is more harmful than beneficial to the human body. As our au-

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: thor (completing the word "author")</meta>

<summary>The author justifies the book's title by comparing antimony to a Roman general; just as a general earns a triumph by defeating enemies, antimony deserves a "Triumphant Chariot" for its unique power to conquer diseases, provided it is prepared correctly.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, Triumphant Chariot, Rome, pharmacology, medicinal preparation, Basil Valentine, disease, simple</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader," following the warning that antimony can be harmful if not prepared correctly by the author.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

author well observes, teaching so many and laudable preparations of the same. These preparations are illustrated in no small measure by the present *Annotations* of the learned *Kirkringius* <note>original: "Kirkringius"; referring to Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693), a Dutch physician and anatomist known for his commentary on this text</note>. Therefore, the purpose <note>original: "end"</note> of translating anew and reprinting this work now for a second time in the *English language* was partly because of the aforementioned *Annotations*, and partly so that such a *laudable work* might not be obliterated by time, and by that means the pious and good intentions of the author be frustrated. 

But more especially, it was translated so that our *talented fellow countrymen*, intent on the *knowledge of natural things*, might find in their own native *language* whatever is needful and necessary for becoming proficient in the *praiseworthy studies of the works of nature*, without being forced to seek *foreign assistance*, as many heretofore have been compelled to do; be-

<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: cause</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the book was re-translated and updated with Theodor Kerckring's annotations to ensure that English readers can study natural philosophy and chemical preparations in their own language without needing foreign sources.</summary>

<keywords>Theodor Kerckring, translation, English language, natural philosophy, scientific education, chemical preparation, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface "To the Reader," following the explanation that many students were previously forced to seek foreign assistance because the secrets of natural philosophy were hidden in other languages.</meta>

...because what they desired to be informed about was locked up in other languages and could be unlocked by only a few people. Those few, if they were capable, were not always willing to do so; and those who were willing, if they were not capable, could not achieve it.

For this very purpose <note>original: "End"</note>, the works of *Geber* <note>original: "Geber the famous Arabian Prince"; likely referring to the Latin works attributed to Jābir ibn Hayyān, the foundational figure of Arabic alchemy</note>, the famous *Arabian Prince* and *Philosopher*, have now likewise been translated into *English*. They are ready for the press and will be published quickly. In the meantime, committing everything to God, we heartily wish everyone well, and specifically wish good success to every *talented student* <note>original: "Ingenious Student"</note> in the *practice of chemical medicine* <term>Chymical Physick: an early form of pharmaceutical chemistry (iatrochemistry) that used alchemical processes to create medicines</term>. *Farewell.*

***

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author concludes the preface by explaining that this English translation overcomes language barriers to alchemical knowledge and announces a forthcoming translation of the works of Geber.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Arabic alchemy, translation, chemical medicine, iatrochemistry, accessibility of knowledge</keywords>

## BASIL VALENTINE
### HIS
# Triumphant Chariot
### OF
## ANTIMONY.

Since I, *Basil Valentine*, am bound by religious vows to live according to the Order of *St. Benedict* <note>The Order of Saint Benedict is a Catholic religious order of independent monastic communities that observe the Rule of Saint Benedict.</note>, I am required to maintain a different level of holiness than the common state of mortals who are busy with the worldly <note>original: "prophane"</note> business of this world. Therefore, I thought it my duty above all things, at the beginning of this little book, to declare what is necessary for the pious alchemist <note>original: "Spagyrist"; a term for an alchemist who focuses on "spagyria"—the art of separating and then recombining substances to create potent medicines.</note> to know. 

Anyone filled with a burning desire for this *Art* <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the secret science and practice of alchemy.</note> must understand what he ought to do and where he should direct his aim. He must lay foundations for the entire matter that are stable. Otherwise, his building might be shaken by the winds and fail, causing the whole structure to collapse into shameful ruin. If it were founded on more firm and solid principles, however, it might have continued for a long time. 

I judged that this warning was, is, and always will be a necessary part of my religious duty. This is especially true since we must all die, and none of us who are alive now, whether of high or low status, shall

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: long</meta>

<summary>Basil Valentine introduces his treatise on antimony by emphasizing that the alchemist must have a firm spiritual and practical foundation, as his religious vows require him to guide others toward stable principles.</summary>
<keywords>Basil Valentine, Order of St. Benedict, Spagyrist, Alchemy, Antimony, Spiritual Foundations, Spagyric Art</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Basil Valentine's spiritual and practical introduction, outlining the five essential pillars of alchemy and detailing the first: the proper way to pray to God.</meta>

->Basil Valentine’s Triumphant<-

...long be seen among the number of men. For it is my duty to entrust these meditations on mortality to future generations, leaving them behind me so that honor may be given to the Divine Majesty and that men may obey Him sincerely in all things.

In these meditations, I found that there were five principal points <note>original: "Heads"</note> to be considered by the wise and prudent observers of our wisdom and art. The first of these is the *Invocation of God*. The second is the *Contemplation of Nature*. The third is *True Preparation*. The fourth is *The Way of Use*. The fifth is *Usefulness and Fruit*. For anyone who ignores these shall never find a place among true *chemists* <note>original: "Chymists"</note> or be counted among perfect *spagyrists* <note>original: "Spagyrists"; alchemists who specialize in the "spagyric" art of separating and then recombining substances to create potent medicines.</note>. Therefore, regarding these five points, we shall discuss them in the following pages and explain them so clearly that the general *Work* <note>The "Work" or "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) refers to the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone or the ultimate medicine.</note> may be brought to light and perfected by a focused and diligent *operator*.

1. The *Invocation of God* must be performed with a heavenly intention, drawn from the depths of a pure and sincere heart and a conscience free from all ambition, hypocrisy, and all other vices related to them—such as arrogance, recklessness <note>original: "Boldness"</note>, pride, luxury, worldly insolence <note>original: "Mundane Petulancy"</note>, oppression of the poor, and other related evils. All these must be rooted out of the heart so that when a man desires to humble himself before the Throne of Grace to obtain bodily health, he may do so with a conscience free from all useless weeds. In this way, his body may be transformed <note>original: "transmuted"</note> into a holy temple of *God* and be cleansed of every impurity. For *God* will not be mocked (as I earnestly warn you), though worldly men, who flatter themselves with their own wisdom, think he can be. *God*, I say, will not be mocked; rather, the Creator of all things will be called upon with re-

<meta>catchword: verential</meta>

<summary>Basil Valentine outlines five essential steps for the alchemist—invocation, contemplation, preparation, usage, and utility—emphasizing that the practitioner must first purge their heart of all vice and pride before seeking God's help.</summary>
<keywords>Basil Valentine, five heads of alchemy, Invocation of God, Great Work, spagyrist, spiritual purity, alchemical ethics</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the first pillar of alchemy: the Invocation of God. It emphasizes that both spiritual salvation and physical health (through medicine) are gifts from the Creator that require a humble and pious heart.</meta>

->Chariot of Antimony<-

[...humble heart,] reverential fear, and acknowledged with due obedience. And there is great reason for this. For what does man have that he must not acknowledge as being received from his omnipotent Creator, whether you consider the body or the soul that operates within the body? Has he not, for the sustenance of the soul, out of his pure mercy communicated to us his eternal *WORD* <note>The "Eternal Word" refers to the Logos or Christ, through whom alchemists believed spiritual transformation was possible.</note>, and also promised eternal *salvation*? Has he not also provided food and clothing for the body, and all those things without which the body cannot survive? 

A man obtains all these through humble prayer to that most excellent Father, who created heaven and earth, along with all things visible and invisible—such as the firmament, the elements, plants, and animals. This is so very true that I am certain no impious man shall ever partake in true medicine, much less in the eternal heavenly bread. Therefore, place your whole intention and trust in GOD; call upon him and pray that he may grant you his blessing. Let this be the beginning of your work so that through it, you may obtain your desired end and finally achieve what you intended. *For the fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom.* <note>A reference to Psalm 111:10 and Proverbs 9:10.</note>

Whoever intends to seek after that which is the greatest of *earthly* <term>original: "Terrene"; things pertaining to the earth</term> things—especially the knowledge of every good thing that is in *creation* <note>original: "the Creatures"; here referring to all created matter in the natural world</note>, which God has liberally granted to men and implanted (in terms of their effective power) in *stones, herbs, roots, seeds, animals, plants, minerals, metals*, and indeed in all things—let him cast away all *earthly* thoughts. Let him reject all that depends on them, hope for purity <note>original: "freeness"</note> of heart, and pray to God with great humility of mind. By doing so, his hope for liberation may at length be turned into true freedom. No man will doubt this if he knows that he alone is God, who delivered

<summary>Valentine argues that true alchemical knowledge and medicine are divine gifts reserved for the pious, emphasizing that prayer and a humble heart are necessary to unlock the secrets of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Invocation of God, Creator, spiritual medicine, Terrene things, piety, alchemical wisdom, fear of the Lord</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Invocation of God," the first of the four pillars of alchemy. It concludes the biblical examples of right-minded prayer and transitions into a commentary by the translator regarding the author's frequent emphasis on religious piety.</meta>

# Basil Valentine’s Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

...delivered Israel from all his enemies; a deliverance he indeed accomplished, not only for Israel, but also for all people who make humble requests to him and pray with a broken heart. 

Therefore, let prayer be the first point of our instruction, which we call the **Invocation of God**. This must not be done with hypocrisy or a fake heart, but with the same faith and trust that the centurion in Capernaum prayed with. It must be done with the same humility and confession of sins that the woman of Canaan possessed, and with the same charity the Samaritan showed to the man wounded on the road to Jericho—pouring wine and oil into his wounds, paying his expenses at the inn, and ordering that he be carefully cared for. Finally, if a person’s Christian charity extends so far that if they obtain what they pray for, they would willingly share it with their neighbor, then they shall undoubtedly obtain wealth and health—the goal of their prayer.

***

*I intend neither to praise nor criticize what the author has stated here at length—and often inserted elsewhere—concerning piety, the worship of God, and the calling upon His name. Let these be judged as signs of his own earnest piety, arguments for his sincerity, and clear marks of his fervent zeal which often boils over in so many repetitions <term>original: "Tautologies"; the redundant repetition of the same ideas</term>. Every man will value this more or less, according to his own opinions and the beliefs in which he was raised from childhood.* 

*My task was to translate the author's writings into Latin <note>The original text by "Basil Valentine" was likely written in German; this commentator is explaining his role in creating the Latin version that served as the basis for many English translations.</note> in a style and order that I thought would not offend the refined taste of the reader. I also aimed to highlight what seems relevant to the work of every chemist and true spagyrist <term>original: "Spagyrist"; a term for an alchemist who practices the "Spagyric" art of separating and then recombining the essential components of matter</term>, without neglecting the rest. Since piety is useful for all things, as the divine scriptures <note>original: "divine Oracles"</note> teach, and the principal exercise of piety is prayer—through which heavenly gifts are obtained from God, the giver of all good things—our author desires that the mind be lifted up to Him, even in the middle of alchemical operations full of labor and toil.* 

*If prayer achieved nothing else, it certainly focuses the mind (drawing it away from all other distractions) and makes it ready for the work at hand. From this, it follows that the mind reflects on many things and considers ideas that would not otherwise enter it if the work were done carelessly <term>original: "perfunctorily"</term> or while distracted with...*

<summary>This page concludes the author's argument that sincere, charitable prayer is the necessary first step to alchemical success, followed by a translator's note justifying the text's heavy religious tone as a means to focus the practitioner's mind.</summary>

<keywords>Invocation of God, Piety, Centurion of Capernaum, Woman of Canaan, Good Samaritan, Spagyrist, Alchemical Labor, Mental Focus</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Invocation of God," explaining how prayer leads to alchemical revelation, and introduces the second pillar of the work: Contemplation.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Chariot of Antimony.</header>
<sig>B 3</sig>

...various conceptions. And so, by the help of prayer, we receive many things which we (if we are not ungrateful) must acknowledge we have obtained from **GOD**. Every person who has at any time entirely devoted himself to this business knows how often this is found to be useful in spagyric <note>original: "Spagyrick"; the alchemical process of separating and recombining the constituents of a substance to create medicine</note> works. Specifically <note>original: "viz." (videlicet); namely</note>, he knows how often those things which he long sought and could not find have been imparted to him in a moment—as if infused from above or dictated by some good spirit <note>original: "Genius"; a guiding spirit or tutelary deity</note>. 

This is also useful for solving all riddles or enigmatic writings. For if you burn with a great desire to know them, that is prayer; and when you focus your mind on this or that, variously debating and meditating on many things, this is cooperation—so that your prayer may not be idle or tempting **GOD**. Yet, your endeavor is in vain until you find the solution. Nevertheless, if you do not despair but persist constantly in your desire and do not cease from labor, at length, in a moment, the solution will appear. This is revelation, which you cannot receive unless you pray with great desire and labor, using your utmost endeavor. Yet, you cannot perceive how the solution itself arose from all those thoughts which were not the solution to the enigma <note>original: "Ænigma"; a puzzle or mystery</note>. 

This unfolding of the riddle opens the mystery of all great things to you. It shows how effective prayer is for obtaining spiritual and eternal things, as well as physical <note>original: "Corporal"</note> and perishable goods. When prayer is made with a heart that is not fake but sincere, you will see that there is nothing more suitable for acquiring what you desire. Let these words suffice concerning prayer, which Basilius <note>Referring to the author, Basil Valentine</note> and all philosophers along with him do not require in vain as an introduction to chemistry <note>original: "Chymiſtry"; here referring to the science of alchemy</note>. For piety is profitable for all works, especially for all great undertakings.

2. Next in order after prayer is **Contemplation**, by which I mean an accurate attention to the business itself. Under this, the following considerations are first to be noted: What are the circumstances of anything? What is the *matter*? What is the *form*? From where do its operations proceed? From where is it infused and implanted? How is it generated by the stars, shaped by the elements, and produced and perfected by the three principles <note>The "three principles" of alchemy are Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt</note>? 

Also, how the body of everything may be dissolved—that is, resolved into the first matter or first essence (which I have already mentioned in my other writings)—specifically, how the final matter may be changed into the first, and the first into the final. *

<note>* What is set down here regarding the true theory of philosophy...</note>

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

<summary>The author explains that alchemical success comes through a combination of sincere prayer, hard work, and divine revelation. He then introduces "Contemplation" as the second step, requiring the alchemist to study the matter, form, and celestial influences of their materials.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, revelation, spagyric, Basilius, alchemy, contemplation, first matter, three principles, planetary influence, enigma</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for alchemical study, moving from the first pillar (Prayer) to the second pillar: Contemplation. It defines "Possible Contemplation" as the theoretical study of nature and the "Impossible" kind as an arrogant attempt to understand the infinite mind of God.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony</header>

*These are summaries <note>original: "Compendiums"</note> of those things which philosophers have—in so many books written about this same pursuit—either revealed or, shall I say, concealed. Pay attention to the words of the author, and you will see that he perfectly understood that Spirit which penetrates all things and rules over all things. Yet, this Spirit is tangled and hidden <note>original: "absconded"</note> within matter and impurities on every side. Once it is freed from these, it returns to the purity of its own substance, through which it produces all things and is "all in all." To write a commentary on this would be a task no less daunting than producing all the books of the philosophers, which were compiled with such careful study and intense debate regarding the Theory.*

This contemplation, which completes <note>original: "absolves"</note> the second part of our instruction <note>original: "Admonition"</note>, is heavenly and must be understood through spiritual reason. For the true circumstances and depth of every thing cannot be perceived in any other way than through the spiritual thought <note>original: "cogitation"</note> of man. This contemplation is of two kinds: one is called "possible," and the other "impossible." 

The latter consists of extensive thoughts that never lead to results, nor do they produce any physical form that can be touched. For example, if anyone should endeavor to comprehend the eternity of the Most High God, it would be a futile and impossible effort. Indeed, it is a sin against the Holy Spirit to so arrogantly pry into the nature of Divinity itself, which is immense, infinite, and eternal, and to subject the incomprehensible wisdom and secrets of **GOD** to human investigation <note>original: "Inquisition"</note>. 

The other part of contemplation, which is possible, is called Theory. This contemplates that which is perceived by touch and sight and which has a formed nature within time. This considers how that nature may be assisted and perfected by the breaking down of the substance into its component parts <note>original: "Resolution of itself"</note>. It examines how every physical body may release the good or evil, the venom or the medicine, that lies hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> within it. It considers how destruction and contraction <note>likely referring to the reduction or concentration of substances</note> are to be managed so that—legitimately and without deceptive tricks <note>original: "Sophistical deceits"</note>—the pure may be severed and separated from the impure. This separation is established and performed through various manual operations, and

...various ways; Some of which are commonly known <note>original: "vulgarly known"</note> by experience, others far removed from common experience. These are: *Calcination*, *Sublimation*, *Reverberation*, *Circulation*, *Putrefaction*, *Digestion*, *Distillation*, *Cohobation*, *Fixation*, and similar processes; all the stages of which are found in operating, and are learned, perceived, and revealed by the same. From this, it clearly appears what is volatile <note>original: "moveable"</note>, what is fixed, what is white, red, black, blue, or green—namely <note>original: "viz."</note>, when the operation is rightly established by the *Artificer* <note>The "Artificer" is the chemist or craftsman performing the work.</note>. For it is possible that the *Operator* may err and turn aside from the right way; but that *Nature* should err, when handled correctly, is not possible.

Therefore, if you should make a mistake so that *Nature* cannot be altogether freed and released from the *Body* in which it is held *Captive*, return again to your path. Learn the *Theory* more perfectly, and inquire more accurately into the method of operating, so that you may find the foundation and certainty in the *Separation* of all things. This is a matter of great concern. And this is the second foundation of *Philosophy*, which follows *Prayer*: for in that, the sum of the matter lies, and it is contained in these words: "Seek first the Kingdom of GOD, and his *Justice* through *Prayer*, and all other things which man seeks in these *Worldly matters* <note>original: "Temporals"</note>, and which he has need of—either for the support or health of his body—shall be added to you."

3. Next to the *Theory*, which searches out the inmost properties of things, follows *Preparation*. This is performed by the work of one's hands, so that some real work may be produced. From *Preparation* arises *Knowledge*—namely, the kind that opens all the fundamentals of *Medicine*. The operation of the hands requires diligent application, for the praise of *Science* consists in experience; but the difference between these is distinguished by *Anatomy*...

Above all things, the Physician ought to know well whether his medicine <note>original: "Medicament"</note> will be weak or strong, and whether it will do good or harm—unless he intends to fatten the churchyard <note>A dark idiomatic expression meaning to fill the graveyard with dead patients.</note> at the cost of his reputation and the risk of his own soul.

*By "Use," the author understands what others call the "Dose." For what will a good medicine profit you if you do not know in what quantity to administer it, so that it heals rather than hurts or kills? To learn this through experience alone is a task full of dangerous risks; however, the discipline or knowledge of doses was first discovered this way and afterward easily taught through words. Where a living teacher's voice is missing, it is safer to be too timid than in any way bold or adventurous. 5. Yet in both the ***Use*** and the ***Effectiveness*** <note>original: "Utility"</note>, this one thing must be considered: namely, whether the disease is an external and open wound, or only an internal and hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> evil. Since the difference between these is great, the method of healing <note>original: "curation"</note> is also not the same. Therefore, the root <note>original: "bottom"</note> of every disease must be known, so that it can be understood whether it can be cured by external remedies alone, or if it must be driven outward from within.

Moreover, if there is an internal <term>Disease: In this context, an ailment or imbalance within the body's systems.</term> which arises and is fed from an internal source <note>original: "Original"</note>, it must never be driven inward by external remedies; for great disadvantage <note>original: "discommodity"</note> will follow from that, and eventually death itself. This may be understood by the analogy <note>original: "similitude"</note> of a tree: for if anyone—while the tree germinates or flowers—repels the fluids <term>Humours: In pre-modern medicine, the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, yellow bile) believed to control health and temperament.</term> back to the interior from which they proceeded to nourish the earth, that tree will be so far from producing the desired fruit from the flower that it will suffocate. This suffocation arises from the violent gathering <note>original: "conjunction"</note> of fluids that cannot find any outlet. 

Therefore, there is a great difference between fresh wounds inflicted by a prick, cut, or any other way, and old wounds which originate from within. Fresh wounds may be perfectly cured by external remedies alone; but for those wounds nourished from within, the external application of oils, balms, ointments <term>Unguents: Healing salves or ointments applied to the skin.</term>, and plasters profits little, unless the internal fountain is stopped from which the fluids flow to the external parts. When the fountain is stopped, the discharge <note>original: "Flux"</note> will cease, and the illness <note>original: "evil"</note> may easily be cured with diet alone. It is no great feat of skill <note>original: "point of Art"</note> to heal a fresh wound; for every countryman can easily do this with raw lard. But to remove all symptoms <term>Symptoms: Visible or felt signs of a disease or injury.</term> that occur in wounds and to dry up the fountain of the illness—this is the true work and the labor of the skilled artist.

Now come here, you physicians <term>Physicians: Medical practitioners; here, Valentine is likely addressing the academic or "Galenic" doctors of his time.</term>, however many of you there are who claim for yourselves the title of Doctor of both medicines—that is, of internal and external diseases. Just as heaven is distant from the earth, so the art of healing internal diseases is vastly different from the healing <note>original: "Sanation"</note> of external wounds. If God grants you your title, that same God will give you a blessing, happiness <note>original: "Felicity"</note>, health, and success. But if your title is vain—devised and assumed only for ambition—then all things will turn out poorly for you. Your honor will fail, and you will prepare yourself for the fires of Hell, which can no more be extinguished than they can be described in words. For Christ said to his disciples, "*You call me Lord and Master, and you are right to do so.*" Therefore, whoever takes on a title of honor should ensure they are acting rightly and not claiming more for themselves than they truly know or have learned—which is the real abuse of such a title.

For someone who intends to call themselves a Doctor of both medicines ought to understand, know, and be well-skilled in both: specifically <note>original: "viz."</note>, internal and external medicine. Nor should they be ignorant of *Anatomy*, so they may be able to show the makeup <note>original: "Constitution"</note> of the body and discover from which organ <note>original: "Member"</note> every disease originates, along with its source <note>original: "Fountain"</note> and cause. They must also understand the remedies used to cure the disease and the specific conditions of external wounds. Good God! Where will that title be found, and what will become of the "Master," when a true test is conducted to reveal the ignorance of these *Doctors* of both medicines?

In the past, long before my time, doctors of medicine treated external wounds themselves and considered it part of their duty <note>original: "Office"</note>. But in our current era, they hire servants to do these things. In this way, the noblest of arts has become a manual labor <note>original: "Mechanick Operation"</note>. Please, do not be offended by this or take it the wrong way; for you yourself will quickly admit—if you seriously consider wounds made by a stab or a cut—that you have as much knowledge about how to cure them as there is in the brain of a dunghill rooster <note>original: "dunghil Cock"</note>. This is the same rooster that children, while learning their ABCs, usually see in the front of his first reading book <note>original: "Frontispiece of their Primer"</note>.

Therefore, I urge all men—no matter their status or condition—who are eager to learn, to seek out the true doctrine from your masters. This doctrine consists first in *Preparation*, and afterward in the *Use*. In this way, you (or they) shall hold your assumed titles with honor. People will undoubtedly have confidence in you, and you will truly be doing them good. Then, you will be able to give thanks to the Eternal Creator sincerely and without pretense.

But let every man think seriously for himself about what he ought to do and what he should avoid, and whether he uses his assumed title justly or unjustly. For anyone who takes on a title must especially understand the nature <note>original: "condition"</note> of that title, why they took it, and what its true foundation is. <page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony</header>

...perhaps [a person] eats food of a most pleasant <note>original: "grateful"</note> taste and odor, well-suited to his natural appetite, yet from it produces excrement endowed with such opposite qualities, yielding a smell so very unpleasant and offensive to nature. There is no other reason for this than natural putrefaction and corruption. The same happens in all aromatic, sweet-smelling things. 

It is the philosopher's <note>In this context, a "philosopher" refers to a natural philosopher or alchemist seeking the secrets of nature.</note> duty to inquire what a smell is, from where it receives its virtues, and how its virtue may be revealed for true benefit. For the Earth is nourished and fertilized by stinking dung, and noble fruit is produced from it. Regarding this matter, there is not just one cause; indeed, our book would swell into a huge volume if we were to even briefly mention every natural generation and mutation. Yet, *digestion* and *putrefaction* are the principal keys to understanding them. 

For *Fire* and *Air* cause a certain *maturation*, which allows a transmutation of *Earth* and *Water* to follow. This is also a specific transformation by which a most fragrant balsam may be produced from foul-smelling dung; conversely, from a most pleasant balsam comes ill-smelling dung. 

But perhaps you will say: why do I provide examples that are so crude and absurd? I confess the example is taken from a cottage rather than a royal court. Yet a wise person who carefully considers these things and dives deeper into the matter will easily understand what such examples suggest—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, that the lowest things are made from the highest, and the highest from the lowest. Thus, a medicine <note>original: "Medicament"</note> is produced from venom, and venom from medicine; the bitter, acid, and corrosive come from the sweet; and on the contrary, something more beneficial comes from the corrosive. 

O good **GOD**, how much of Nature is hidden <note>original: "absconded"</note> from men, so that she seems to refuse to be fully seen by us? But since You have ordained such...

But to return to my philosophy of *Antimony*, I would have the reader understand, above all else, that all things contain within themselves operative and life-giving <note>original: "vivificative"</note> spirits. These, inhabiting the body, feed and nourish themselves and are sustained by the body. The elements themselves do not lack these spirits, which (by the permission of the living *GOD*) have their habitation in them, whether they be good or evil. Humans and animals have in them a living, operating spirit, and when this leaves them, nothing but a carcass remains. In herbs, and all things bearing fruit, a spirit of health <note>original: "Sanity," meaning soundness or healing power</note> exists; otherwise, they could not be reduced to medicinal use by any preparation. Metals and all minerals are endowed and possessed with their own incomprehensible spirit, in which the power...

> *What follows seems somewhat confused, according to the opinions of certain theologians who have held various views on Spirits residing in Fire, Air, and other Elements—consigning them to the Eternal Fire of Hell, etc. All this, along with *Basilius* <note>original: "Basilius"; the Latin name for Basil Valentine</note>, we leave as unknown to the judgment of Divine Knowledge. into various forms until the time of their judgment. However, whether a final <note>original: "definitory"</note> sentence should be pronounced against them yet, I leave to the Providence of the Divine Majesty, from whom nothing is hidden. 

There are other spirits, lacking speech, which cannot show themselves visibly in action. These are the spirits that live in animals—including in humans and the like—as well as in plants and minerals. Nevertheless, they possess within themselves a hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> and active <note>original: "operative"</note> life. They reveal and discover themselves through their effective power of operation, which they contain and carry within them. They most clearly provide testimony of their healing power <note>original: "virtue of healing"</note> whenever that power is extracted from them by the help of Art <note>In alchemy, "Art" refers to the specialized chemical and laboratory processes used by the alchemist to refine substances.</note>, once it has been accurately separated from their physical bodies. 

In the same way, the effective spirit and active power of *Antimony* manifests its gifts and distributes them among people, once it is first released from its own body and freed from all its bonds. In this state, it is able to penetrate <note>meaning to move through physical matter at a molecular or spiritual level to effect a cure</note> and is made fit to be applied to those uses which the craftsman <note>original: "Artificer"</note> intended during its preparation.

But the artist and *Vulcan* <note>In alchemy, "Vulcan" represents fire or the god of the forge; the term refers to the controlled heat of the furnace used in chemical reactions.</note> must work in harmony: the fire provides the separation needed for active power, and the craftsman shapes the matter. Just as a blacksmith uses one kind of fire and iron is his only material, which he intends to use for forming various instruments—for sometimes he makes a spit from it, at another time horseshoes, at another time a saw, and eventually countless other things—each of these serves the purpose the smith intended, even though the material he prepares for so many different uses is only one. 

Similarly, various works can be made from *Antimony* for different uses. In this process, the artist is the smith who shapes it; *Vulcan* is, as it were, the key that opens it; and Operation and U- <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "Utility".</note>

[U]tility <note>The word is partially cut off from the previous page; "utility" or "subtility" is likely.</note> give experience and knowledge of the life. Oh! if foolish and vain men had but ears to hear, and true eyes with understanding—not only for hearing what I write, but for understanding the secret <note>original: "Arcanum"</note> and the knowledge of its use; they would surely not swallow those unhealthy <note>original: "insalubrious"</note> and murky <note>original: "turbid"</note> potions, but would hasten to these clear <note>original: "limpid"</note> fountains and drink from the Well of Life.

Therefore, let the world know that I shall prove those pretended doctors, who seem to be wise, to be mere fools and idiots, and cause many unlearned men (who are studious disciples of my doctrine) to become true doctors indeed. Therefore, I here solemnly summon <note>original: "cite"</note> and invite all men who earnestly aspire to knowledge—with a cheerful mind, good conscience, and certain hope—to embrace and become witnesses of our doctrine, and to accurately study my writings and instructions. For by doing so, at length, they (having gained possession of what they sought) will praise and commend me after death, making my mortal name immortal through their perpetual remembrance of my praises as long as the world endures. 

But if, when I am dead, anyone is pleased to start a debate <note>original: "disputation"</note> in the academic schools against me, my writings will fully answer all his objections; and I am assured my disciples will never forget the benefit received from me, by which they will obtain the mastery <note>original: "Empire"</note> of Truth. That truth was always sufficient for me, and always will be for them, to suppress a lie until the world's end.

Also, let the well-meaning and sincere observer of the Art <note>In alchemy, "the Art" refers to the specific laboratory processes used to refine and transform matter.</note> know that there are two kinds of *Antimony* which are very different from each other. One is beautiful, pure, and has the properties of gold; it contains a great deal of *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of fluidity, volatility, and the "spirit" of a substance.</note>. The other, which has much *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the principle of oiliness, combustion, and the "soul" of a substance.</note>, is not as compatible with gold as the first, and is distinguished by beautiful, long, and white shining streaks.

Therefore, one is more suitable for medicine and *alchemy* <note>original: "Alchimy"</note> than the other. Just as the flesh of fish is compared with the flesh of other animals—although both are, and are called, "flesh," each differs greatly from the other—so it is with the varieties of *antimony*. Many indeed write of the internal properties <note>original: "Interiour virtue"</note> of *antimony*, but few of them ever taught the true foundation of the properties with which it is endowed <note>original: "endued"</note>, or discovered how it receives them. Consequently, their *doctrine* is based on words alone and lacks any true foundation; thus, they lose the results <note>original: "Fruit"</note> they hoped to gain from such writings. 

Writing truthfully about *antimony* is a task that requires deep meditation, a broad-minded approach, and knowledge of its many *preparations*. One must also understand its true *soul* <note>In alchemy, the "soul" refers to the essential, active spirit or concentrated essence of a substance believed to provide its medicinal power.</note>, in which all its usefulness resides. Once this is understood, you can give an undisputed judgment on what evil or good, poison <note>original: "Venome"</note> or medicine, is hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> within it. It is no small matter to search into *antimony* through a true examination <note>original: "Examen"</note>, to fundamentally penetrate its *essence*, and through earnest study to achieve final knowledge of it. In this way, its poisonous nature <note>original: "Venenosity"</note>—which unskilled men ignorantly complain about—can be removed. It may then be transformed and prepared into a better state, becoming a medicine fit for use and free of poison.

Many practitioners <note>original: "Artists"</note> intending to analyze <note>original: "Anatomize"</note> *antimony* have troubled, twisted, and tormented it in various ways that can hardly be described in words, let alone believed; yet, when the matter is truly examined, they accomplished nothing. For they did not seek its true *soul*, and therefore they could not even find the artificial <note>original: "feigned"</note> soul they were looking for. These men can be excused only by saying they deserve the name of "Stupid"; yet that stupidity is not to be tolerated, because they do not desire to learn or be better informed, either here or elsewhere.

*Antimony* is endowed <note>original: "endued"</note> with all four primary qualities <note>The "four first qualities" refer to the Aristotelian system: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>: it is cold and moist, and again it is hot and dry, and it adapts itself to the four seasons of the year. Also, it is both volatile and fixed <note>In alchemy, "volatile" substances evaporate or change easily when heated, while "fixed" substances resist heat and remain stable.</note>. The volatile part of it is not free of venom, but the fixed part is free from all poisonous nature <note>original: "venenosity"</note>. This is so very strange that it may be considered one of the seven wonders of the world, about which so many writers have spoken without knowing themselves what they wrote. There has been no man before me—and at this day there is none found—who has so thoroughly learned the power, virtue, strength, operation, and effectiveness of *antimony*, or so profoundly penetrated into all its energy, so that nothing remains hidden or undiscovered, or cannot be brought to light through experience. ...because the natural heat of the stomach is too weak to process <note>original: "concoct"</note> and digest that raw <note>original: "crude"</note> body. From this, my dear friend, you may conclude that since *antimony* has a greater venom and a coarser <note>original: "groſs"</note> mineral body than animal flesh (as I have already shown by the common example mentioned above), it will also prove more dangerous if used raw, without preparation, separation, and cooking <term>coction</term> <note>the process of boiling or heating a substance to change its properties</note> by fire. Indeed, it will be and remain a poison <note>original: "Venom"</note> that will suddenly kill the sick person. 

Therefore, the poisonous nature <note>original: "Venenoſity"</note> of *antimony* must be removed so that it can never be turned back into poison. This is done in the same way that *wine*, once it has been turned into *vinegar* through fermentation <note>original: "putrefaction and corruption"</note>, never again yields any *spirit of wine* <note>alcohol or ethanol</note>, but always is and remains *vinegar*. On the contrary, if only the spirit of the *wine* is separated and the watery part <note>original: "Aquoſity"</note> left by itself, and that same spirit is afterward concentrated <note>original: "exalted"</note>, it will never be changed into *vinegar* in any way, even if it were kept for a hundred years. It will always remain *spirit of wine*, just as *vinegar* remains *vinegar*.

This transformation <note>original: "Transmutation"</note> of *wine* into *vinegar* is a wonderful thing, because something is produced from *wine* that was not previously in its plant-based <note>original: "vegetable"</note> essence. In this, it should also be noted that in the distillation of *wine*, the *spirit* comes out first; but (on the contrary) in distilling *vinegar*, the watery residue <term>phlegm</term> <note>in early chemistry, the tasteless, watery, or inactive part of a substance produced by distillation</note> comes first, and the *spirit* afterward, as I have shown above in its proper place, where I also mentioned this example. Therefore, *spirit of wine* makes bodies <term>volatile</term> <note>easily evaporated; in alchemy, substances that "fly" from the fire or do not resist heat</note> because it is volatile itself. However, *spirit of vinegar* stabilizes <note>original: "fixeth"</note> all medicines <note>original: "Medicaments"</note>, whether they are made from minerals or plants, and makes them solid. In this way, they can take hold of stable <note>original: "fixed"</note> substances and drive out stubborn <note>original: "fixed"</note> diseases.

* Consider and observe these things diligently; for this principal Key is of great concern. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Therefore, the preparation of antimony consists of the "Key of Alchemy," by which it is dissolved, opened, divided, and separated—such as in *calcination* <note>reducing a metal to powder by heat</note>, *reverberation* <note>heating a substance in a furnace where the flame is deflected downward</note>, *sublimation* <note>heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then back into solid form</note>, etc., as we declared above. It also consists of extracting its essence and in vivifying <note>making active or "alive"</note> its mercury; this mercury must afterward be precipitated into a fixed powder. Likewise, by art and a proper method, an oil <note>original: "Oyl"</note> may be made from it, which is effective enough to completely consume that new and unknown disease <note>referring to syphilis, often called the "French disease" during this period</note> which the *French*, in their warlike expeditions, brought into our regions. ...long steeped in water until they are able to sufficiently open and dissolve <note>original: "resolve"</note> themselves (as I diligently observed during my travels to England and Holland as a young man). This stage is called *putrefaction* and *corruption*. 

Once this "Key" has been used, the water is drained from the grain. The soaked <note>original: "macerated"</note> corn is then laid in close heaps and left for a proper amount of time until it begins to heat up naturally. As the grains germinate from this heat, they stick to one another. This stage is called *digestion*. 

When this is finished, the grains that stuck together during germination are separated and dried, either in the air or by the heat of a fire, to harden them. This is called *reverberation* and *coagulation*. 

When the corn has been prepared in this way, it is taken to the mill to be broken and ground into small pieces. This is called *vegetable calcination* <note>the process of reducing organic matter to powder through heat or grinding</note>. 

Afterward, by boiling <note>original: "cocting"</note> these grains over a fire, their more noble spirit is extracted, and the water absorbs <note>original: "is imbibed with"</note> it. This would not be possible without the preparation mentioned before. In this way, the plain <note>original: "crude"</note> water is converted into beer. This operation—though I describe it simply—is called *distillation*. 

When hops are added to the beer, they act as its *vegetable salt*, which protects and preserves it from any opposing forces <note>original: "Contraries"</note> that might try to spoil <note>original: "corrupt"</note> it. The Spaniards and Italians do not know this method of boiling water into a drink by extracting the spirits from the grains. Even in my native land of Germany, near the Rhine, few people are skilled in this art.

After all these tasks are completed, a new separation is made through *clarification* (filtering the drink). This is done in the following manner: a little yeast <note>original: "yest"</note> or ferment is added, which triggers an internal motion and heat within the beer. This causes it to rise up within itself, and (by the...

<page-num>26</page-num>

With the help of time, the *separation* of the dense from the rare and of the pure from the impure occurs. By this means, the beer acquires a constant power <note>original: "virtue"</note> in its action, so that it penetrates and achieves all those ends for which it was made and brought into use. Previously, this would have been impossible because the spirit—the active force <note>original: "Operator"</note>—was hindered by its own impurity from performing its proper work.

Does experience not teach the same regarding wine? Until the time comes when the impurities can be separated from it, wine cannot perform its work as perfectly and effectively as it does after the separation of the pure from the impure. This is made manifest by the effects of drunkenness; for unsettled and unpurified beer or wine does not release as much spirit for intoxication as it does after clarification. But I will say no more of this. 

After all the steps mentioned above, a new operation may be started through "vegetable sublimation" <note>the alchemical process of purifying organic matter through heat</note> to separate the spirit of the wine or beer. By distillation, it is prepared into another drink called *Burning Wine* <note>original: "Burning Wine"; high-proof alcohol or aqua vitae</note>, which may also be made from the lees <note>sediment</note> or dregs of wine and beer. 

When this is done, the active power is separated from its own physical body. The spirit, being extracted by fire, abandons its own useless, dead "habitation" <note>the leftover solid matter</note> in which it was comfortably housed before. Now, if this *Burning Wine* or spirit of wine is rectified <note>purified by repeated distillation</note>, an "exaltation" <note>an increase in potency and purity</note> is achieved by distilling it often. By a certain method of operating, the pure part (free from all phlegm <note>the watery, tasteless part of a distillate</note> or wateriness) may be so concentrated and, as it were, condensed, that one measure of it may achieve more than twenty or more measures could have done before. For it causes intoxication sooner and is swift, volatile, and subtle in its ability to penetrate and act.

# The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

...who desire to be taught by my writings and hope to obtain riches and a true medicine from **Antimony**, I ask that you would not carelessly read my intention. In these writings, not one letter is written in vain, and every word has a specific and unique meaning for your instruction.

*Come here, you traveler, and stop your journey. Do not despise or lightly pass over this repetitive, but not irrelevant, warning. Frequently return in your mind to this description of beer <note>The author uses the brewing and fermentation of beer as an analogy for alchemical processes</note>; search, contemplate, and weigh all things. If you are still blind in this light, I do not know of any eye-salve <term>original: "Collyrium"; a medicinal lotion or ointment for the eyes</term> that will help you. Believe, read, meditate, labor, and save yourself the use of so many chemical books which distract you with the errors of various paths; this one book tells you everything.*

Indeed, I here solemnly affirm that there are many words dispersed here and there in my writings to which, if the reader pays proper attention and knows the fundamentals where the principal points of the matter are situated—and, as it were, buried—he will have no cause to regret often turning over these same pages. Instead, he will value every word as much as a piece of gold coin. For you know that although the examples I propose may sound harsh, as they are delivered in a crude manner, they contain within them something excellent and of great importance. Yet, I am not ambitious here to gain authority or praise for my writings; that is not my business, nor would it become me. For when the results of these operations are brought to light, they will acquire enough praise for themselves. I purposely and willingly produce examples that are so crude and common because the power of **Antimony** and its true virtue—deeply and profoundly hidden <note>original: "absconded"</note> in its innermost parts—must be searched out. Here I will provoke a great argument among the learned; for I know they will doubt what my words signify, questioning whether what I affirm and write is even possible, and they will be divided into several opinions on the matter. I do not wonder that they persist in that opinion, since the theory of such preparations is absolutely unknown to them; they have not the slightest thought of what leads to the knowledge of these more profound mysteries. # Chariot of Antimony

...into a better state. For you (I mean you doctors) must admit to me that your purpose is to transform that evil, which caused the disease in the first place, into a better state. 

Consider this: will you not also grant that if any evil exists in the materials from which medicine is made, that same evil must be converted into a better state? This is so that the medicine may perform its work much better and act more powerfully and profitably without any significant danger. 

However, because the method by which a person ought to proceed in these preparations is known to very few as yet—or proven by experience—only a small number of people will support the opinion I affirm and publicly profess here. For many will be carried away by the larger crowd, crying, "*Poison, Poison!*" When I hear these voices, they remind me of those wild outcries by which the Jews demanded that the Savior and Redeemer of the world suffer the punishment of the cross. They often cried out, "*Crucify him, Crucify him!*" They proclaimed him to be the highest, most immediate, most pernicious, and cursed poison <note>original: "Venom"</note>, when in fact he was the noblest, supreme, most glorious, and most beneficial medicine of our souls. It was he who was to deliver us from the death of sinners, from the Devil, Hell, and all misfortunes. 

Although those proud Pharisees and lawyers neither could nor would understand this, he nevertheless was then—and will be until the end of the world, and after it for all eternity—the same superior medicine. Neither the Devil, nor Death, nor the very gates of Hell, nor any creature, however powerful or perverse, can do anything to overthrow this truth.

<meta>This page transitions from the historical etymology of antimony to a discussion on the principles of toxicology and sympathetic medicine, using the legendary properties of the unicorn's horn as a scientific analogy for how substances attract or repel venom.</meta>

# Chariot of Antimony.

Now let any reader consider and observe, as a thing worthy of note, that true **unicorn's horn** <note>original: "Unicorns-horn". In the early modern period, "unicorn horn" usually referred to the tusk of a narwhal, which was highly prized for its supposed ability to detect and neutralize poison.</note>, unadulterated by any trickery <note>original: "sophisticated by no fallacy"</note>, repels all venom from itself. Nor can it take in or draw to itself anything of venom, as is proven by experience. Let a living spider be placed in a circle made of **unicorn's horn**, and it cannot leave this circle or crawl over the horn, for the spider shuns whatever is opposed to venom. But if the circle is made of venomous material, it is not to be doubted that the spider will leave it and crawl over that venom, which is like itself. 

Note this experiment: make a hollow piece of silver and place it in water so that it floats like a boat. Place venom within it, then hold a piece of true unicorn's horn as near to it as you can without touching it. By its spiritual power <note>original: "spiritual Virtue"; in alchemy, "virtue" refers to the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance.</note>, the unicorn's horn will drive the silver boat away from itself. The silver will fly away like a duck swimming on the water that takes flight when it perceives the snares of a bird-catcher <note>original: "Fowler"</note> ready to entangle it. 

But on the contrary, nature in a wonderful manner loves and follows its own kind, as appears by this example: Put a little piece of pure and fine bread in a dish full of water so that the bread floats. Hold a piece of true unicorn's horn close to it, yet without touching it; and if you slowly move the unicorn's horn, the little piece of bread will follow it. Nature so much loves its own kind, and so much hates what is unlike itself, that the one flees while the other follows. 

Therefore, let our **doctors** <note>In this context, "doctors" refers to learned physicians or scholars of medicine.</note> consider that venom, by a certain magnetic power, attracts venom; and those things which are free of venom likewise draw to themselves things that are pure and lacking any poisonous quality.

Therefore, venom may be removed in two ways: first, by its contrary, which resists venom, as already

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how substances interact with venom, transitioning from the previous page's focus on "repulsion" (the unicorn's horn) to the principle of "attraction" (like-cures-like), using the chemistry of soap and the freezing of an egg as scientific analogies.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot.

...as has already been told about the **unicorn’s horn**. Secondly, it works by similarity, when venom—through a certain magnetic power—draws other venom to itself. However, the venom intended to heal a similar venom must first be so prepared that its poisonous nature is transformed into medicine. By its own attractive power <note>original: "virtue"; in alchemy, "virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal power or active property of a substance</note>, it must draw the other poison to itself and expel it. 

You have a very clear example of this in **soap**: it is made of oil and other fatty ingredients which seem to be—and indeed are—more likely to soil linen than to clean it. But because during the boiling of the soap, especially with the help of salt, a certain separation and preparation occurs, the soap is rendered most capable of drawing to itself and washing out all grime and filth from linen and other things. In the same way, venom can be prepared beforehand and adapted so that it is no longer venom, but a medicine <note>original: "medicament"</note>. It draws all other poison to itself, casts it out, and restores the person to their original <note>original: "pristine"</note> purity and health.

Now, since our discussion has led us this far and we have begun to reveal so much about nature, those truly studying medicine—though previously ignorant of this—may clearly know what good or evil lies hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> in nature, and what is poisonous versus what is harmless. This is something that doctors have not yet discovered because of their own lazy <note>original: "supine"</note> negligence. To demonstrate and confirm this truth, it will be helpful to provide certain experimental examples that may reveal the truth and refute the false opinions of others. 

Place an **egg** that has been frozen <note>original: "congealed with Cold"</note> by the winter cold into very cold water. Let it lie there for a sufficient amount of time, and ice will stick to the outside of the eggshell, but the cold will be extracted from the egg itself, and the egg will be restored to its original vigor and wholeness <note>original: "entireness"</note>. By this means, such wounds will be so prepared that they may be perfectly healed by other medications <note>original: "Medicaments"</note> applied to them. Alternatively, if linen clothes are frequently moistened in frogspawn and dried just as often, and that linen is then cut into small pieces and applied to wounds, they achieve the same effect as the aforementioned powder would have done.

This hidden *Sulfur* <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible principle of a substance</note>—the inner fire—is revealed by that forceful strike and is fueled by the air until it burns effectively. > *You who read this simple comparison of steel and flint, restrain your amazement and seriously ask yourself: can a method be found to extract a substance from stone and cold iron, where only one grain—or even a hundredth of a grain—can quickly transform a great mass of raw matter into the most beautiful and precious of all things? Yes, even into a "fire" most beneficial for humanity? This is possible and is done daily when that which is "fixed" <note>stable or solid</note> is made "volatile" <note>vaporous or spirited</note>, and the volatile is made fixed again. For example, when iron is dissolved in nitric acid, if you suddenly pour oil of tartar upon that solution, you will have a difficult time keeping the glass from breaking; for the opposing substances, like gunpowder, catch fire and shatter the glass. Of all these things, our academic doctors <note>original: "Gown Doctors"</note> know nothing at all; therefore, they have no other defense for their ignorance but silence alone.

<meta>This page continues Basil Valentine’s exploration into the chemical and spiritual origins of antimony. Having established it as a subject worthy of study, he now explains its physical composition and its relationship to the "Three Principles" of alchemy: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</meta>

->Basil Valentine's Triumphant<-

Maturity. *Antimony* is nothing other than a fume, or (as I may otherwise call it) a mineral vapor which is generated from above by the stars, and afterward brought down and refined <note>original: "deduced and digested"</note> by the elements into a formal solidification <note>original: "Coagulation"</note> and maturity. Here it is to be noted that *antimony* has acquired its essence, virtue, power, operation, and quality from the same principle, root, and essence from which common mercury <note>original: "Vulgar Mercury"</note> is produced; yet with a firmer solidification, so that it is brought to a harder essence than the "living" or "running" mercury of the common sort possesses. 

The reason for this is because it has taken from the three principles <note>In alchemy, Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury were believed to be the three fundamental ingredients of all metals and minerals.</note> a little more of the substance of Salt than common mercury. For although it has the smallest portion of Salt out of all three principles, it has still taken more of the essence of Salt than common mercury, which is why it has reached such a solidification. Hardness in everything comes from Salt, which common mercury lacks. 

For common mercury has a very small part of Salt, but within it, in a spiritual manner, is implanted <note>original: "insited"</note> a certain hotter *Spirit of Sulfur*; therefore it always flows and cannot be brought to a solid state unless by the help of other metallic spirits. These spirits, endowed with very great virtues, are chiefly found in the *Matrix of Saturn* <note>original: "Matrix of Saturn"; this refers to lead ore, as Saturn was the alchemical name for lead.</note>, without which it cannot be fixed <note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy means to make it solid and stable so that it no longer evaporates or flows when heated.</note>, unless by him who possesses the *Philosopher's Stone*. By that stone, its three principles may be brought to a harmonious equality, and then it acquires such a body as will melt, flow, and withstand the hammer like all other metals. Otherwise, mercury is and will remain fluid mercury until its volatility is removed in this way. 

Hence it is known that all animals and all vegetables are too weak to fix mercury into a malleable <note>original: "malleable"; capable of being hammered or pressed into shape without breaking.</note> substance (as many have tried in vain to do) because they do not have a metallic nature. *Mercury*, within and without, is

is nothing other than mere fire; therefore, it is not combustible by any fire. And whatever can now be made of gold may then also be made of it by Art, because after solidification <note>original: "Coagulation"</note>, it is altogether like gold: for it has one and the same root, stock, or origin as gold.

But since I do not intend in this place to discourse more extensively of *Mercury*, and intend only simply (yet truly) to describe the very foundation of *Antimony* from true and certain principles, I will cease to speak of *Mercury* and proceed to a further explanation <note>original: "Declaration"</note> of *Antimony*. Yet, whatever I have proposed through this comparison <note>original: "Similitude"</note> regarding *Mercury* \*, as an introduction for further consideration, is not written in vain or to no purpose. It is intended so that the very beginning of *Antimony* may be more clearly understood, which (as I showed before) received its origin, as it were, mercurially along with it.

Therefore, our author *Basilius* does not invite your attention in vain. All the light I am able to add to this clarity would obscure rather than illustrate it; therefore, along with him, I advise you to pay attention.</gloss>

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

This substance is extracted from the elementary earth by the superior stars, as if by a sidereal <note>sidereal: relating to the stars or constellations</note> distillation of the macrocosm <note>macrocosm: the great world or universe as a whole</note>. This sidereal hot infusion, descending upon inferior things with an airy-sulfurous property, acts and operates in such a way that a certain power and virtue is implanted spiritually and invisibly within those metals and minerals. This fume afterward resolves itself within the earth into a certain water, from which mineral water all metals are henceforth generated and ripened to their perfection. 

From this process proceeds this or that metal or mineral, depending on which of the three principles <note>The three principles of alchemy: Sulphur (the soul/flammability), Mercury (the spirit/fluidity), and Salt (the body/solidity)</note> acquires dominion. They vary according to whether they have much or little of Sulphur and Salt, or an unequal mixture of their weight. This is why some metals are fixed—that is, some are constant and stable—while others are volatile and easily changed, as is seen in **Gold**, **Silver**, **Copper**, **Iron**, **Tin**, and **Lead**. Besides these metals, other minerals are also generated from the three principles according to the communication and participation of their unequal weights: such as **Vitriol** <note>original: *Vitriol*; iron or copper sulfates</note>, **Antimony**, and many other **marcasites** <note>marcasites: iron sulfide minerals, often used as a general term for crystallized ores</note>, or other **electrums** <note>electrums: naturally occurring alloys of gold and silver, or specific metallic compositions</note> and **minerals**, which for the sake of brevity we omit here.

But **Gold**, in its *astrum* <note>astrum: the "star" or essential celestial virtue of a substance</note> and beginning, was imbued with a much more perfect **Sulphur** and a much more perfect **Mercury** than all other metals and minerals. Therefore, its operative virtue is much more potent and more efficacious than the *astrums* of other metals. Indeed, all virtues whatsoever that are dispersed among other metals—and many more besides—are found in the *astrum* of **Gold** alone. Moreover, I say that when that one thing is brought to further maturity by fire, it contains more perfection than all metals and minerals together. There is only one mineral, of which I have often made mention already, in which is found a Sulphur of
