So also, there are two kinds of metals found in which this dominance <note>original: *predominancy*</note> powerfully triumphs. I have neither the will nor the desire to write about those at this time; instead, I wish to keep within the bounds I have set for myself in treating the essence of ***Antimony***, which I intend to speak about now.

Therefore, ***Antimony*** is a mineral made from the vapor of the earth changed into water. This spiritual, sidereal <note>sidereal: relating to the influence of the stars or constellations</note> transformation <note>original: *transmutation*</note> is the true star <note>original: *astrum*</note> of antimony. This water is first extracted from the elementary earth by the stars, and afterward by the element of fire (which resides in the element of air). Through coagulation, it is formally changed into a tangible essence. In this tangible essence—from which antimony is formally made—there is a great deal of predominant ***Sulphur***, not as much ***Mercury***, and the least amount of ***Salt*** among the three. Yet it absorbs enough ***Salt*** to become a hard and brittle <note>original: *immalleable*</note> mass. 

Its primary quality is dry and hot, or rather burning. It has very little coldness and humidity, unlike common mercury. In physical <note>original: *Corporal*</note> gold, there is also more heat than cold. This should be enough regarding the matter and the three fundamental principles of antimony, and how it is brought to perfection by the ***Archeus*** <note>Archeus: in Paracelsian alchemy, the vital force or "internal chemist" that governs the formation of minerals and the health of the body</note> within the element of earth.

Yet the students of chemistry <note>original: *Chymistry*</note> should not think that the philosophical reasoning I have presented is absolutely necessary for them. Nor do they need to be anxious to know in which center the "star" <note>original: *astrum*</note> of antimony rests, or from what root it flows. Instead, they should desire to learn its usefulness and practical application—namely <note>original: *viz.*</note>, how it ought to be prepared and reduced to its proper state so that they may understand its virtue, power, and operation. # Basil Valentine’s Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

In the past, so many things have been written about this, and to this day it is mentioned and spoken of by all people of all statuses and conditions. For the learned, as well as the unlearned, hope to have their ardent and insatiable desire satisfied by this; therefore, I will not detain the reader with ambiguous discourses or tedious delays, but simply teach everything that I—through great study and diligence (which I have often applied to ***antimony***)—could find out concerning the essence of ***antimony***. 

For no one, because of the shortness of life, can know or search out all its secrets <note>original: *Arcanum’s* — Latin for hidden mysteries or deep secrets of nature</note>. This is because in the preparation of ***antimony***, one new wonder follows another perpetually: one stage <note>original: *Degree*</note> succeeds a former stage, color follows color, and one virtue, power, or operation always manifests itself as greater than another.

And to begin here, I say that ***antimony*** is mere poison <note>original: *Venom*</note>—and not the least kind of poison, but such a kind by which you may destroy men and beasts, so venomous a power is diffused through the whole substance of this mineral. From this arises the common outcry of all people. For the common people, unskillful doctors, and all those to whom the foundation of true medicine is unknown, proclaim with one voice: "Poison! Poison!" They say—as I myself admitted above—that poison lies in ***antimony***. "For this reason," they say, "let us dissuade everyone from its use, for it endangers health and life." 

Therefore, doctors residing in the courts of princes advise monarchs, princes, and other powerful rulers <note>original: *Potentates*</note> not to use ***antimony***. Other scholars <note>original: *Scholasticks*</note> cry out, "Beware! Do not admit ***antimony*** into medicinal use in any way, for it is mere poison!" The inhabitants of cities and villages follow their lead. And this far-reaching clamor so influences the majority of mortals that ***antimony*** in these days of ours is very ill-spoken of, and no...

The first reason why **GOD**, the supreme Lord of the stars and the maker of heaven and earth, has presented manifest <note>original: "open"</note> venom to us, especially in minerals, is that by this divine order He might show us His wonders and powerful works for the distinction of good and evil—just as in the Law He prescribed to us the knowledge of that which it is our duty to do, namely: to choose the good and avoid <note>original: "eschew"</note> the evil. A second reason is that by this we might comprehend and understand the distinction between evil and good, and at length learn to exterminate malice and restore <note>original: "reposit"</note> goodness in its place. For **GOD** does not will that man should perish and be destroyed, but that he should depart from evil and come to an amendment of life, so that destruction may be driven far away from his soul. So, to us His creatures, with wonderful appropriateness <note>original: "conveniency"</note> has He proposed good and evil, which is found both in the commandment of the Word <note>referring to Scripture</note> and in the work of the creature <note>referring to the natural world</note>; so that we may choose what is profitable and good for health, and shun what is evil and harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note>.

Thirdly, venom is also made by the stars, when contrary oppositions and conjunctions of them happen, by which the elements are infected so that they become the cause of plagues <note>original: "pestilences"</note> and other venomous diseases in this world; this is also to be understood regarding comets.

Fourthly, venom is made from things contrary <note>original: "repugnant"</note> to each other, as when anyone kindles a deadly poison in himself through anger or sadness; also when a man drinks while being excessively <note>original: "above measure"</note> hot.

Fifthly, among venoms may be numbered weapons with which anyone is slain; in that case, the abuse of arms is venom to that man. But when anyone uses arms for the just and blameless defense of his body—the purpose for which they were invented—then they may be accounted a certain kind of medicine.

Lastly, the cause of venom may be demonstrated by *Nature* in this manner: whatever *Nature* resists is *Venom*, and that is because it fights against *Nature*. For example, when anyone eats such *food* as his *stomach* cannot bear, then that *food* is *venom* to him, for it is contrary <note>original: "repugnant"</note> to *Nature*. On the contrary,

# Chariot of Antimony

If anyone eats food that is compatible with their stomach, that food serves as medicine to them.

But poison is primarily drawn to bodies within the earth while they are in the form of a certain "Mercurial Essence" <note>original: "Mercurial Effence"; in alchemy, the "spirit" or volatile principle of a metal</note> (I am speaking here of the poison found in minerals). At this stage, it exists in an immature, raw, and poorly "digested" <note>original: "digested"; in alchemy, digestion refers to the process of maturing a substance through heat</note> form, which is hostile to nature and difficult to process. Because such a Mercurial Essence is not yet perfected or matured, it passes through the whole body as a raw, immature, and indigestible mineral. 

Just as if men were to eat raw grain, it would be so difficult for the stomach to digest that a significant weakness of the body would follow. For our internal natural heat is too weak to bring that grain to proper digestion <note>original: "Concoction"</note> and perfection. Grain, which receives its maturity from the Sun (the "Fire of the Great World" <note>The Macrocosm</note>), must afterward be thoroughly cooked by human fire (the "Minor Fire") so that it may be digested by the human body (the "Microcosm" <note>Man as a "little world" reflecting the universe</note>). 

As we previously mentioned regarding the boiling of meat until it is ready, the same must be understood of ***Antimony***. Because it is still raw and has not been thoroughly "cooked" <note>original: "cocted"</note> within the earth to a state of stability <note>original: "fixedness"; the quality of being resistant to change by heat</note>, the human stomach (so to speak) is too weak to tolerate or retain it. This is proven by clear experience—namely, that all ***purgatives*** <note>original: "Catharticks"; medicines used to induce vomiting or bowel movements</note>, whether mineral, animal, or vegetable, are poisonous because a certain "Mercurial" volatile matter still dominates them. This volatile spirit is the reason why other substances within the human body are expelled. 

However, this does not mean the actual root of a disease is captured; that can only be achieved by the stability <note>original: "fixedness"</note> of a medicine. For every medicine that is thoroughly stabilized searches out deep-seated <note>original: "fixed"</note> diseases and eradicates them. Purgatives that are not stabilized cannot do this; instead, they only carry away some of the waste products <note>original: "Spoyl"</note> from the disease; or they may be...

But as to metals, the black is assigned to Saturn <note>The alchemical name for lead</note>, the red to iron, the yellow to gold, the green to copper, the blue to silver, the white to Mercury <note>Quicksilver</note>, and its mixture of various colors is attributed to Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for tin</note>. 

But as all the colors of all metals and precious stones are clearly found in antimony; so also all the powers and virtues of medicine are shown in it no less than the colors mentioned above. However, to extract all these colors from it is not the labor of just one man. For our life is restricted by narrower limits than will permit one man, through his own labor, to thoroughly learn whatever Nature keeps concealed and hidden <note>original: "absconded"</note> in her bosom. 

In one certain way of preparation, an acid and sharp fluid <note>original: "Humour"</note> is drawn forth from antimony by distillations, like true, perfect vinegar. By another way, a shining red color is prepared, sweet and savory, like purified honey or sugar. By another way, a bitterness like wormwood proceeds from it; otherwise, a certain sharpness <note>original: "Acrimony"</note>, like some salt-oil. Thus, one nature always follows another. 

Again, by sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</note>, it is driven to the Olympic Mountains like a flying eagle <note>An alchemical metaphor for the volatile vapors rising during heating</note>—red, yellow, and white. Also forced down by "descent" <note>A method of distillation where the vapor is forced downwards into a receiver</note>, it yields diverse colors and preparations. Also by reverberation <note>Heating in a furnace where the flames are reflected or "reverberated" onto the substance</note>, a metal is made from it like common lead. Likewise, a transparent glass is created—red, yellow, white, black, and endowed with other colors—all of which, notwithstanding...

<summary>Valentine explains that antimony possesses the colors and medicinal virtues of all other metals and gemstones, though its secrets are too vast for one person to fully master. He describes various chemical transformations of antimony, including its conversion into vinegar-like acids, sweet red substances, and colored glass.</summary>

#### Chariot of Antimony

...nevertheless, they are not safe to be used in medicine unless they are first tested by another examination <note>original: *Examen*</note>. It is also resolved into rare and wonderful oils, which are various and manifold. Some of these are perfected with additions, while others are made without the mixture <note>original: "mixtion"</note> of any other substances; some are taken internally, while others are only applied externally to common ulcers and wounds. It provides us with so many different extractions, varied with so many colors, that it would tire even a Delphian Apollo <note>A reference to the Greek god Apollo, associated with the Oracle at Delphi and profound wisdom, suggesting the task is beyond human capacity</note> to describe them all. But indeed, all the transformations <note>original: "Mutations"</note> of its nature, which are discovered through the "Gate of Fire" <term>Gate of Fire: The alchemical process of using heat and furnaces to transform substances</term>, will be best revealed by its own results. From it is made living mercury and sulphur which burns like common sulphur, so that even gunpowder might be made from it. From it is also made a true and natural salt, and many other things are prepared from the same source.

Therefore, we begin to speak of its preparations, such as its essence, magistery <term>Magistery: A highly potent medicinal substance or "master-remedy" prepared through alchemical skill</term>, arcanum <term>Arcanum: A secret, powerful remedy believed to hold the "secret of nature"</term>, elixir, and specific tincture. In these, you must employ all diligence and care, especially when I shall declare to you in my writings the "Stone of Fire" and its preparation, along with various other secrets and hidden remedies <note>original: "Arcanums"</note> which are scarcely known to the world at all. These have been little regarded since the Egyptians, Arabians, and Chaldeans <note>Ancient civilizations famous in the early modern period for their supposed mastery of alchemy and secret sciences</note> died—those who once practiced these arts. Despite this neglect, the use of these arts is very great for searching out the very fountain of true medicine and all other works related to it.

Now, diligently mind and consider with profound meditation all the following preparations, each succeeding the other as I shall reveal them. For there is not one included here that does not have its own unique usefulness; rather, every one of them is useful, according to how it is ordained in its state. A fixed medicine of antimony...

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot

...testimony, expels fixed diseases and eradicates them; but *antimony* that is not fixed—as when it is crude <note>original: "crude"; meaning raw or in its natural state</note> and not prepared—opens and purges the stomach only, but does not touch the root of the disease. Therefore, I will set about the preparation of all that pertains to *antimony*, and discover all the keys of its preparation, which now (as if by a new birth <note>original: "Nativity"</note>) are brought to light and revealed by fire, in the same state to which they were ordained by God their *Creator*. This unlocking and preparing of mineral *antimony* is performed by various methods and ways, through the management and governance of the fire and with much manual labor, from which proceed the operation, virtue, power, and color of the medicine itself. And since *antimony* appears to the eye as a raw black color mixed with a little whiteness, I will first speak of its destructive alteration, which consists in *calcination* <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder</term> and *reduction to ash* <note>original: "Incineration"</note>, and that is performed as follows:

Take Hungarian or other *antimony*, the best you can get; grind it, if possible, into an extremely fine <note>original: "Impalpable"</note> powder. Spread this powder thin all over the bottom of a calcining pan, round or square, which has a rim around it the height of two fingers' thickness. Set this pan into a calcining furnace, and apply to it at first a very moderate charcoal fire, which you should afterward increase gradually. When you see a smoke beginning to rise from the *antimony*, stir it continually with an iron spatula without stopping, as long as it gives off any smoke from itself. If, during calcining, the *antimony* melts or hardens into lumps <note>original: "concrete into Clots"</note>, then remove it from the fire, and when it is cold again, reduce it to a fine powder. As before, calcine it, continually stirring as we said, until no more smoke will ascend. If need be, repeat this operation as often and as long as necessary, until that *antimony*, when put into the fire, will

<page-num>63</page-num>

[...When you see that the ashes] neither fume nor clump together into clots, but resemble white and pure ashes in color: then the calcination of *antimony* is correctly finished.

Put this *antimony* thus calcined into a goldsmith's crucible set in a furnace, and intensify the fire with bellows, or put it into a wind furnace <note>A furnace designed to create a strong natural draft of air to reach high temperatures</note>, applying enough heat so that the *antimony* flows like clear and pure water. Then, so that you may certainly and without fail prove whether the glass made from it has been sufficiently cooked <term>Cocted: From the Latin 'coquere', meaning to cook or process with heat to achieve chemical maturity</term> and has acquired a transparent color, put a long, cold iron rod into the crucible. Some of the glass will stick to the iron; strike it off with a hammer and hold it up against the light to see whether it is clear, clean, and transparent. If so, it is well made and perfectly mature.

Here, let my reader—who is unskilled and just a beginner in this art—know (for I write not to men skilled in this art who have often experienced the powers of fire, but to candidates, *beginners* <note>original: "Tyro's"</note>, and the studious disciples of the *spagyric science* <term>Spagyric Science: An alchemical term referring to the process of separating and then recombining the components of a substance to create a medicine</term>; because making glass of *antimony* is a common thing, well known to many), let him know, I say, that every glass—whether made of metals, minerals, or any other matter—must be thoroughly processed by fire to its due maturity. This ensures it has a clear and transparent color and is suitable for further preparation for medicinal use. This translucent and pure maturity is only achieved by *fire* <note>original: "Vulcan"; the Roman god of fire, used here to represent the chemical heat of the furnace</note> in its secret and hidden nature. Therefore, let every man know, consider, and remember this.

<note>* Basil <note>original: "Basilius"</note> correctly says that he writes to the beginners of this art, because he begins with the glass of antimony—that is, from the very rudiments of chemistry—and teaches how to make it so accurately that no man, however ignorant, can be deceived by it. Yet the ancients have quite often experienced how often they erred in the practice <note>original: "Praxis"</note>; for such people, I have written these commentaries—</note>

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant

...ries. For my part, let everyone please themselves in their own writings; surely, I believe I have offered something that posterity will always thankfully accept. For although I most diligently read Basilius <note>Basil Valentine, the purported 15th-century monk and author of this alchemical work</note> and other masters of the Art of Arts <term>Art of Arts: A traditional name for Alchemy, considered the highest and most difficult of the chemical sciences</term> for several years and followed them as exactly as possible in my labors, I still committed so many errors (the memory of which fills me with horror). I lost so much money and was so often forced to correct those errors with hard work that I feel compassion for all those who would enter this path, motivated by an earnest desire to help their neighbors. I have no respect for others who aim at nothing but riches and would make so noble an art subservient to avarice—the worst of all vices; let them suffer the losses they deserve. 

But you, who are endowed with a more noble spirit: first seek the Kingdom of God—which is established and spread through charity toward your neighbor—and all other things that other men so impiously seek shall be added to you spontaneously by the bounty of God. I need no great arguments to persuade anyone to read these commentaries, for a person's own business will sufficiently warn them when they see how often I can save them from great expenses with a single word or a most simple observation <note>original: "animadversion," meaning a critical remark or helpful correction</note>—costs they have too frequently incurred while laboring without success. I candidly confess to you, studious reader, that if these manual operations had been as sincerely shown to me as I reveal them here, I would have saved a great sum of money. I very often erred when I over-eagerly pursued certain processes described by others, and through that vain effort, I lost several thousand florins <note>Florins: A gold or silver coin; the author is highlighting the significant personal wealth he sacrificed to learn these secrets</note>. 

Yet I seldom had to repeat twice any of the operations that our author (who, of everyone I know, has written most sincerely and openly) has included in this book. I will not establish a basic introduction <term>Tyrocinium: From the Latin for "apprenticeship," this refers to a foundational textbook or introductory course for beginners in a craft</term> to chemistry here, as other authors well known to beginners have already done. Instead, I am willing to help through advice those who have long since been able to release this subject from its bonds and who, with the most fervent desire, aim to reach the goal set before their eyes and minds. I do this so that neither "slippery blood" on the path nor "Entellus" lying prostrate might hinder them from gaining the proposed reward: riches and health <note>These are classical references to Virgil’s *Aeneid*; Entellus was an aging boxer who slipped on the blood of a sacrificed ox but rose to win. The author uses this to describe the risk of slipping or failing just as one is nearing the alchemical goal.</note>.

When your *antimony* is converted into glass using the method we have taught, take a smooth, broad copper platter or dish. Heat it hot in the fire—otherwise, your matter will splatter <note>original: "flie out"</note>. Then pour in the fluid matter as thinly as you can, and you will have pure, yellow, transparent glass of *antimony*. This is the best way of preparing glass of *antimony* by itself <note>original: *per se*</note> without any additions. This glass, above all others, is endowed with the greatest virtue and power, which it manifests...

# Chariot of Antimony

...manifests after its further preparation. This is what I call *Pure Glass of Antimony*.*

---

* This is now the common and well-known way of making *Glass of Antimony* <note>original: "Glaſs of Antimony," a transparent, amber-colored substance created by roasting antimony ore and melting the resulting ash</note>, which is useful in many operations; however, to administer it to the sick without distinction is a task full of danger and peril. It does indeed sometimes succeed happily, but the accidental health of a few is not so important that one should expose the life of any man to peril. For I have seen a sick man who, after taking only half an ounce of the infusion, vomited and purged beyond measure and died soon after. 

From this come those tears and those outcries against Chemists—as if the impious recklessness of some false chemists <note>original: "Pseudochymists"</note> should be blamed on the Art itself. These false chemists do not care how many houses they fill with funerals, provided that one or two people who are healed will spread their fame, allowing them to hear themselves called "Doctors" and rob the simple-minded of their money. The reason for this great danger is that all the emetic <note>original: "Emetick," meaning it induces vomiting</note> force of antimony is contained in its fixed salt, which holds all its toxicity <note>original: "Venomoſity"</note>. Weak natures cannot overcome this, and therefore they receive more harm from its venom than good from its health-giving <note>original: "Salutiferous"</note> virtue. 

But this danger should not deter healthy men from using antimony, since they see that even when mixed with venom, it often produces healthy effects. They should rather reason this way: if that health-giving virtue were freed from its harmful qualities, what good would it not do, or what diseases would it not heal? Therefore, behold, I offer you here such *Glass of Antimony* as I use often myself, which may be used by any man without any danger of a fatal disaster <note>original: "mortal Cataſtrophe"</note>.

Take pure *Glass of Antimony*, made as Basilius <note>Basil Valentine</note> teaches here; melt it in a crucible <term>crucible: a ceramic or metal container in which metals or other substances may be melted or subjected to very high temperatures</term>, and keep it in a molten state <note>original: "in flux"</note> until a third of it is consumed. Then let it cool and grind it into an impalpable powder <term>impalpable powder: a powder so fine that its individual grains cannot be felt by touch</term>. Over this, pour highly rectified spirit of wine <note>original: "Spirit of wine," meaning concentrated ethanol or high-proof alcohol</note> until it stands three finger-widths above the powder. Close the vessel firmly and circulate <term>circulate: an alchemical process of heating a liquid in a sealed vessel so that the vapors condense and flow back down, a form of refluxing</term> the matter for three months. Then, by distillation, remove the spirit of wine—or, if it is tinted red (which it always will be if you have operated correctly), simply pour it off and keep it separate, for it is an excellent medicine. Put the remaining substance back into a crucible, allow it to melt, and then cast it into whatever forms you wish. For it can take whatever shapes you desire, which may even be set in rings and worn on the hand. Its medicinal use is as follows:

Put this *Glass* for one night in two ounces of cold wine, and in the morning, let the sick person drink that wine. You will find very good success, for it purges gently; and if nature is inclined to bring the matter upward, it performs that action moderately, causing gentle vomiting. Only note this: the prescribed dose must be reduced according to the strength, age, and constitution of the patient. Here, reader, kindly accept this first admonition offered to you, and expect to find more if you willingly and intently read through what follows.

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

For there are other forms of glass prepared from *antimony* by the addition of *borax* and other substances, in the following way:

Take one part crude *antimony* and two parts *Venetian borax*. Put these together into a crucible. Place the vessel in a wind-furnace <note>A furnace designed to create a strong natural draft for high temperatures</note> or intensify the fire with bellows to make the mixture flow, so that they may be thoroughly and perfectly mixed together. Afterward, pour the mixture as thinly as possible into a heated copper pan or dish, as described in the previous preparation. You will find your *antimony* beautiful and transparently clear, * like a *pyrope* <note>original: "Pyropus," a deep red gemstone like a garnet</note> or a *ruby*, provided you follow the proper and accurate method and manage the fire correctly.

<note>* The *warning* to which our *author* attributes the *success* of this process is that you must use an extremely strong fire, like that required for melting *gold*. Without this, you cannot achieve the *redness* of a *pyrope*.</note>

The *redness* can be extracted from this red *glass* using *spirit of wine* <note>original: "Spirit of Wine," which is concentrated ethanol or alcohol</note> *, and through long-continued *circulation* <note>A process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel</note> in the fire, be perfected and made into a most excellent, beneficial, and effective *medicine*.

<note>* This should not be done with common *spirit of wine*, but with *philosophical spirit*, which I prepare for extracting this *tincture* as follows:
Take 4 ounces <note>original: "℥iiij"</note> of *sal ammoniac* <note>ammonium chloride</note> that has been sublimated <note>purified by heating into a vapor and cooling back to a solid</note> three times. Take *spirit of wine* distilled over *salt of tartar* <note>potassium carbonate, used here as a drying agent</note> so that it is perfectly dehydrated <note>original: "dephlegmated"</note>. Put them together in a vial and place them in the heat of *digestion* <note>gentle, steady warmth</note> so the spirit may fully absorb the *sulfur* or "fire" of the *sal ammoniac*. Then distill the mixture three times using an *alembic* <note>a type of still</note>, and you will have a true *solvent* <note>original: "Menstruum"</note> with which to extract that *redness* from the *glass of antimony*.</note>

The *tincture* <note>a concentrated medicinal extract</note> of this *glass* can also be extracted with its own proper *vinegar*, and through further processing it is perfected and becomes a most excellent *medicine*.

### Basil Valentine’s Triumphant [Chariot of Antimony]

...expressing my praises and publishing the same, as if with a great sound, through all parts of this inferior orb <note>The Earth or the material world</note>. This will be a necessary consequence of the gratitude of my disciples when they shall see with their eyes, touch with their hands, and with their understanding comprehend that I, to their great profit, have declared the very truth to them without deceit and made them the heirs of a memorable testament.

Therefore, the first separation of the sulfur <note>original: "Sulphure"; in alchemy, this refers to the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance</note> from its body, and the extraction of the tincture <note>A concentrated liquid extract containing the medicinal essence</note> from its salt, is performed in this manner: Take pure glass of *antimony*, as I taught you to make it, without the addition of any other thing. <insert>
* What I shall advise here is short but very profitable; without it, what the author appoints cannot be done. You can never bring the body to the required fineness by beating or grinding in a mortar alone, nor can you grind it upon a porphyry stone <note>A hard volcanic rock used by painters and alchemists to grind materials into extremely fine powder</note> in its dry state. Therefore, first beat it in a mortar, and afterward mix it with distilled vinegar so that it has the consistency of a soft, paste-like matter. Then grind it upon a porphyry stone as painters grind their colors, and undoubtedly you will obtain your desire.
</insert>

Put this powder into a glass with a plain, flat bottom called a cucurbit <note>A gourd-shaped vessel used in distillation and digestion</note>, and pour upon it strong, well-rectified <note>Purified or concentrated through distillation</note> vinegar. Then set the vessel in a digestive fire <note>A steady, gentle heat intended to simulate the warmth of a stomach</note>, or if it is summer, expose it to the sun, stirring it twice or thrice a day. Digest it in that temperate heat until the vinegar takes on a yellow color inclining to redness, like the color of the cleanest and most well-purified gold.

# Chariot of Antimony.

...being more wary, from the very beginning I began to stir the substance with a wooden spatula five or six times a day, or more often. You may imitate this same method, if you are wise—not only here, but also in the higher preparation of antimony and in every extraction of tincture from antimony.

Then pour off this clear and pure extraction, and pour on fresh vinegar. Repeat the operation as long as the vinegar is tinted, and until no more tincture can be extracted. Filter all these extractions mixed together and put them into a glass body <note>A cucurbit or flask</note> with its head <note>The alembic or still-head</note> attached, and by a water bath <term>B. M.</term> <note>original: "B. M." or *Balneum Mariae*; a water bath used for gentle, even heating</note> distill off the vinegar until a yellow powder, leaning toward redness, remains at the bottom. Upon this powder, pour distilled rainwater many times, and as often distill it off again, always pouring on fresh distilled rainwater. Repeat this labor until the powder remains sweet and pleasant <note>original: "grateful"; meaning palatable or free from the harsh, acidic taste of the vinegar</note>,

---

> *Our most sincere author here does not deceive you, but he does conceal a certain physical technique. If you pay attention to me as I clearly reveal it, the work itself will never fail to fulfill your desire. If you have a large amount of tincture, you must use a large <term>cucurbit</term> <note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</note>; if you have a little, a smaller one will serve. For if you take a larger vessel than your tincture requires, the vinegar must necessarily have a hotter fire to make it rise because of the height it must travel, or it cannot be distilled. In maintaining such a powerful fire, there is great danger of ruining <note>original: "Corrupting"</note> the tincture itself. Here also another warning is required: namely, after two-thirds are distilled off, you must change your vessel and put the remaining substance into a smaller glass body. From there, distill off the vinegar until the remaining matter reaches the thickness of a poultice <note>A soft, moist mass or paste</note>. Also take care, as <term>Basilius</term> <note>Basil Valentine, the purported author of this text</note> seems to suggest, that you do not distill the vinegar until it is completely dry, lest the tincture be entirely ruined by scorching <term>adustion</term> <note>The process of burning or charring through excessive heat</note>.*
>
> *Despite all this precaution I have just given, I could not help but labor for a whole year to little purpose, often repeating this tincture in a useless effort. Because of this, I was almost as often weary of chemistry <term>chymistry</term> out of desperation; for my tincture had no effectiveness as a medicine because it was merely a dead head <term>caput-mortuum</term> <note>original Latin: "dead head"; the worthless residue left after distillation or sublimation</note>, tasteless and of no value. Therefore, consider how little any process benefits you—whether set down in writing or received from a friend by word of mouth—unless you put your hand to the work and practically learn every detail of the task required for successful operation. See also how generously I deal with you in revealing that which, through my own ignorance, cost me great trouble and expense. The physical technique which is required for purifying <term>edulcorating</term> <note>To free from soluble substances or acids by washing; literally "to sweeten"</note>...*

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

*The way to handle this soft, pap-like substance remaining in the bottom is as follows. Pour distilled water over this matter and gently distill it off using a water bath <note>original: "Balneo"</note>. When you have repeated this a third time, you will find the water coming off sweet; you must observe this timing with very great diligence. For if you are deceived in this, your work is at an end, all your labor is lost, and you shall obtain nothing but a <term>Caput Mortuum</term> <note>Latin: "dead head"; the useless, inert residue left over after an alchemical process</note>. For as soon as twenty, or at most thirty drops of sweet water come forth, an acidity appears again and distills out. The unwary, judging this to be the acidity of the vinegar added earlier, continue distilling in the hope that the water will become sweet. But because this is the acidity of the Antimony itself—which immediately follows the sweet water once the vinegar is extracted—continuing the distillation destroys the entire medicinal power of the Antimony, leaving nothing but a tasteless residue. Therefore, be more cautious: as soon as this sweet water comes forth, stop the distillation. Take out the pap-like substance residing at the bottom and, putting it into another glass, allow it to dry in the heat of the sun; or else evaporate all its moisture with a very gentle fire, so that it remains as a dry powder. When you have avoided this danger, then proceed.*

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Grind this powder upon a <term>Marble</term> or <term>Glass</term> slab that has first been made hot. Then put it in a <term>Glass Body</term> <note>A cucurbit or flask used for heating and distilling</note> and pour upon it enough of the best rectified <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>Highly concentrated alcohol or ethanol</note> to stand three fingers' thickness above it. Then set it in a <term>Digestive heat</term> <note>A gentle, steady warmth, similar to the internal heat of a living body</note>, as described above, to extract the **Tincture of Antimony**. This tincture will be deeply colored and an amazingly beautiful red; it will deposit a certain earth or dregs at the bottom.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This extract is sweet, pleasant, and so very effective in **Medicine** that no one who has not experienced it will believe it. The dregs at the bottom retain the toxicity <note>original: "Venenosity"</note>, while the extract contains only the medicine, which experience has taught to be a profitable remedy for both humans and beasts. For if three or four grains of this medicine are taken, it expels **Leprosy** <note>original: "Leprosie"</note> and <term>Syphilis</term> <note>original: "Gallick Lues," literally "the French disease"</note>, purifies the **Blood**, drives away **Melancholy** <note>A state of deep sadness or depression, then thought to be caused by an imbalance of "black bile"</note>, and resists all venom. Whoever suffers from shortness of breath, difficulty breathing, or sharp pains in the sides <note>Pleurisy or similar chest pains</note> may be cured by the use of this medicine, which effects many other

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

...and instilled <note>original: "insisted"</note> in them such a medicine, that deserved thanks and praise should rightly be given. In all old, malignant, and corrosive wounds, use this extract or balsam of **antimony**, and it will never fail you in your time of need. And you yourself, following after me, will write a formal speech of praise <note>original: "Encomium"</note> and publish its virtues, by which miserable mortals may be made happy when it is applied externally. For lupus <note>original: "the Wolf"; a skin-eating disease</note> and cancer yield to it; rot in the bones, and malignant ulcers corroded and pierced with worms flee from it. It restores one to original health and maintains its effect when its fixed form <note>the non-volatile part of the substance</note> is properly used internally and other suitable means are properly applied externally.

\* If surgeons <term>original: Chirurgions</term> would believe our author here, with what great care would they preserve this balsam for themselves, and with what great benefit and frequency might they use it? For I, offering my own judgment, must say that Basil <note>original: Basilius</note> falls far short here in expressing its proper praise; for it performs even more than he claims for it. One short case study <note>original: "History"</note>, taken from the volumes <term>original: Centurys; books containing a hundred case studies</term> of my medical observations, will confirm the truth of what I have said. 

A certain woman, about forty years of age, suffered great pains <term>original: dolours</term> in her left breast for seven consecutive years, which were accompanied by a tumor and hardness. Those surgeons and physicians with whom she consulted all agreed that her disease was cancer. She was also judged to be suffering from cancer by the opinion <term>original: Censure</term> of that famous practitioner who very commendably and successfully practiced surgery <term>original: Chirurgy</term> at Oriscotus (a village near Dukes-Wood <note>likely referring to 's-Hertogenbosch in the Netherlands</note>) and attracted a vast number of people to him. After she had spent three months trying in vain to heal this disease, he strictly declared that her breast must be amputated <term>original: "cut off"</term>, or the disease could not be removed <term>original: extirpated</term>. 

The woman, deciding she would rather endure all the pains of the disease than undergo such a cruel and inhumane remedy, came to me. Upon examining her breast, I found it completely inflamed and twice the size of the other, with an abundance of watery fluids <term>original: thin Humors</term> flowing to the wound. I intended to do everything I could rather than let this miserable woman die. Thinking of this balsam, I resolved to see whether that which had fulfilled its author's promises in other diseases would fail me here. Therefore, to the sick woman waiting for my answer, I said that within eight days I would tell her whether there was any hope of a cure without amputation; and there—

# Chariot of Antimony

...consequently gave her this remedy to anoint her breast with; and, strangely enough, within the space of two days, the infection <note>original: "the Matter"</note> reached maturity and a proper consistency. Because of this, with a special joy, I blessed and praised the Lord who had granted such great power <note>original: "virtue"</note> to this balsam.

I also have two ways of reducing glass of **antimony** into an oil through distillation (as they call it) using an alembic <term>an alchemical still used for distilling liquids</term>.

Take glass of **antimony**—as it is made from the ore <note>original: "Minera"</note> of **antimony**—finely powdered, and extract its tincture with distilled vinegar. Afterward, remove <note>original: "abstract"</note> the vinegar from it and wash <term>original: edulcorate; to purify by washing away salts or acids</term> the remaining powder. Then pour on spirit of wine <term>ethanol or high-proof alcohol</term>, with which you extract the tincture, and circulate it in a pelican <term>a specialized alchemical flask with two side-arms that allow the liquid to condense and flow back into the body</term> that is well-sealed, for an entire month. Afterward, distill it by itself <note>original: "per se"</note>, without any additions, with a certain unique skill <note>original: "Dexterity"</note>; from this, you will receive a wonderfully pleasant and sweet medicine in the form of a red oil, from which the "Stone of Fire" may later be created.

***

\* This is the work, this the labor <note>original Latin: "Hic opus, hic labor est"; a famous line from Virgil’s Aeneid</note>; very few true "Sons of Art" <note>a term for dedicated alchemists</note> (whom **Apollo** <note>the god of healing and truth</note> loves) could extract this tincture using an alembic. As **Basil** <note>original: Baſilius</note> says, there is a need for a specific, unique manual procedure. I sought this tincture for many years and at last—God favoring me—I found it. Do you want me to reveal it to you through a riddle <note>original: Ænigma</note>? I see that you wish me to, so take this mystery as follows: **Alciatus** <note>Andrea Alciato, a famous 16th-century author of emblem books</note>, when painting a dolphin coiled around an anchor, wrote these words: *Make not too much haste* <note>original Latin: "Festina lente"</note>. Value this advice, not only throughout your life but also in this specific matter, as it will be very profitable to you; for "the hasty bitch," as the saying goes, "brings forth blind puppies." 

Therefore, I warn you again and again to have wings prepared for your substance by **Juno** <note>representing the element of Air</note>, **Bacchus** <note>representing the spirit of wine/alcohol</note>, and **Vulcan** <note>representing the element of Fire</note>; but, as you value your life, do not allow it to fly off suddenly. Instead, deliver it to **Mercury** <note>representing the principle of volatility or the liquid metal itself</note> to be instructed by him, so that it gradually becomes accustomed to flying. Indeed, bind it with a cord, lest—like a bird escaped from a cage and beyond your reach—it ignorantly approaches too near the sun and, like **Icarus**, has its feathers burned and falls headlong into the sea. But after you have held it for its proper time, loosen its bonds so that it may fly and reach those fortunate islands to which all "Sons"...

# Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

*...where all practitioners of the Art direct their vision, and where all Adepts <note>original: "Adeptists"</note> aim to arrive, as if reaching their desired and long-sought harbor. Here, lover of the Art, do not be offended or angry with me as if I were deceiving you (you who are so eager for knowledge of this secret) by showing you an apple like that of Tantalus <note>original: "Tantalick Apple"; a reference to the Greek myth where Tantalus was cursed with fruit that stayed just out of reach</note>. What should I do? In this case, I offer you advice. Would you have me cast pearls before swine? Would you have me expose the mystery to everyone—a mystery which the ancients kept so holy and were not permitted to reveal, except to the worthy sons of the Art? You yourself would mourn nothing more, and would even curse me for doing so. Those who understand me, understand the Art; and for those who are chemists, I have opened the way. If they travel it diligently, they may arrive where they desire to be. No one ever revealed this to me so clearly; but by reading the writings of authors, laboring intensely, and trusting in God without fainting or despairing (which is a most effective kind of prayer), I at length achieved what I have. Study and be diligent so that you may understand. For he who knows how to make tinctures volatile <term>capable of being evaporated or turned into gas</term> is already admitted into the very inner chambers and secret rooms <note>original: "Penetrate and Conclave"</note> of the chemical Art; because the method for all other mysteries is the same. Read the fables, search through the riddles, and consider the parables of all wise men; they all point here, and all say the same thing. Compare the parables of others with my own enigma <note>original: "Ænigma"</note>, and mine with theirs, so that you may understand how much light I have added to everything, and how easy I have made the path to those serene temples of wisdom.*

---

This oil is the **quintessence** <term>the purest and most concentrated essence of a substance</term>, and the highest thing that can be written about **antimony**. As you may find in my former writings, I have made a short declaration of **antimony**, in which I also showed that there are four **instruments** required for its preparation, and the fifth is that in which **Vulcan** <note>the Roman god of fire; here referring to the alchemical fire or furnace</note> has made his home. 

Understand it this way: four preparations must be made before it can be perfected, and the fifth is the usefulness and effect of the work within the human body. 

1.  The first labor is **calcination** <term>heating a substance to a high temperature to oxidize it or remove impurities</term> and **liquefaction** <term>melting a solid into a liquid</term> into glass. 
2.  The second is **digestion** <term>the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat</term>, by which **extraction** is performed. 
3.  The third is **coagulation** <term>turning a liquid into a solid</term>. 
4.  The fourth is **distillation** into oil, and after that, separation follows. 

Finally, there is **fixation** <term>making a substance stable and resistant to heat</term> by the ultimate coagulation, through which the matter is brought to a transparent <note>original: "perlucid"</note> **Stone of Fire**. For this stone to operate upon metals, it must be fermented to acquire its ability to penetrate them;

## Chariot of Antimony.

But it is not as powerful as that ancient *Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: "Stone of Philosophers"; the legendary substance capable of transmuting metals and curing all ills</note>, because it is not universal, but only colors <note>original: "tingeth"; refers to the alchemical process of "tincture" or transformation</note> specific things. Regarding this, more shall be spoken toward the end of this book when we treat of the *Stone of Fire*.

This distilled **Oil** <note>In alchemy, an "oil" refers to a concentrated liquid essence extracted from a substance</note> of which we have now spoken, accomplishes everything that a physician needs to know and requires for his cures.

<margin>Had I not known Basilius <note>referring to Basilius Valentinus, the purported 15th-century alchemist and author</note>, I would have thought him to be acting like a deceiver or a wandering quack <note>original: "Medicaster"</note> toward you. However, the truth itself speaks for him so often that I religiously hesitate to doubt his promises. For whatever I have experienced—and there are very few processes in this book which I have not tried, since he was my teacher, friend, and patron from the very beginning—I have found to be so effective beyond the author's promises that it seems to me he has been modest in declaring the virtues of his medicines, lest by praising them he should be thought to be commending himself too much. Yet I shall not undertake to comment extensively on their praiseworthy virtues here in his place. Let him who does not believe it make a trial so that he may know. Whoever is deterred from experiencing the truth by his own disbelief will suffer enough punishment for his offense by lacking the benefits of the medicine. For this Oil, if used correctly at the right time, is a truly universal medicine. Consider, I pray, what this means: once the body is first purged of gross and raw fluids <note>original: "crude Humours"; according to the medical theory of the four humors</note> and general medicines are used (as you may remember I previously advised), this Oil is a universal medicine for healing all curable diseases. For chemists <note>original: "Chymists"</note> are not so mad or conceited because of the goodness and virtue of their medicines that they do not recognize some diseases as incurable. For who can restore any of the vital organs <note>original: "Principal Members"</note> once they have been consumed by rot? Yet I would not have all diseases judged incurable by these, our chemical remedies, which are everywhere commonly condemned as such. For example, how often have I restored the crystalline lens <note>original: "Crystalline Humour"; the lens of the eye</note> after it was lost? Who does not judge that impossible to restore? But there will be a more fitting place to write of these and similar things. I say only of this medicine that it heals fevers of every kind—yes, even the *Quartan* fever itself <note>a fever where paroxysms occur every fourth day, often associated with malaria and notoriously difficult to treat</note>, which is that ancient shame of physicians—and it shows wonderful effects in long-term illnesses <note>original: "Chronical Distempers"</note>. Here, among many of my observations, I will give you only one experiment. In the year 1655, a young woman, aged twenty-one and swollen to an enormous size with dropsy <note>edema; an accumulation of fluid in the body</note>, came to me for help. I used only this for her medicine. ### Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot

<note>The author is describing a patient with dropsy, or edema.</note> Her swelling lessened by half an ell <note>original: "Ell"; a unit of measurement roughly 45 inches</note> within that space of twenty days. As I said, she also passed a great deal of urine (provoked by the same medicine), but her sweating was wonderful.

Note, my friend and lover of Art, that this *Oil*, whether you prepare it yourself or receive it from another who has prepared it, does not imitate other diaphoretics <note>medicines that induce sweating</note> in its operation. Other medicines, when used, will provoke sweats in their first dose. However, if this is given to a patient whose body is obstructed with humors <note>bodily fluids believed to control health</note>, the first dose does nothing but gently open the passages so that sweats may be produced. The next day, it causes a gentle and healthy breathing of sweat; the third day, it sweats moderately; but from the fourth day onward, it causes such an abundance of sweat that the waters proceeding from it run through the bed onto the floor. Here, there is a need for a true physician; Hercules' club will be of little use if it is not in the hand of Hercules himself.

The dose of it before coagulation <note>thickening into a solid</note> is eight grains taken in wine. It makes a man very young again and delivers him from all melancholy. Whatever grows and increases in the body of man, such as the hair and nails, falls off, and the whole man is renewed—just as a *Phoenix* is renewed by fire (if such a mythical bird, which I name here only as an example, can actually be found anywhere on the earth). This medicine can no more be burned by fire than the feathers of that unknown *Salamander* <note>a mythical lizard believed to live in fire</note>. It consumes all symptoms in the body like a consuming fire, to which it is deservedly compared; it drives away every evil and expels everything that <term>Aurum Potabile</term> <note>literally "Potable Gold," a legendary alchemical life-extension elixir</note> is capable of expelling. The *<term>Astrum</term> of <term>Sol</term>* <note>The "Star of the Sun," referring to the spiritual essence of Gold</note> only excels every other medicine in the world when it is rightly prepared to perfect fixation. For the *Astrum of Sol* and the *Astrum of Mercury* arise almost from the very same blood of their mother, and from one original source of life-giving health.

* Let no man here waste his own gold prodigally or rashly now that he hears of the *Astrum of Sol*, nor expose himself to such great hazard as to enter a perilous combat with common <term>Mercury</term>. In the <term>chemical</term> <note>original: "Chymical"</note> world, another *Sun* shines, and another *Mercury* attends on *Jupiter*. Yet the chemical Sun or Mercury mentioned here is not common gold, but something more excellent than all gold; yes, more potent than every...

## The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony

Mercury, although a figure of myth and fabled to be capable of restoring the dead to life, represents the "Gold" and "Living Silver" <note>original: Argent-vive</note> of the Philosophers, which Basilius <note>Basil Valentine, the purported author and legendary alchemist</note> hints at here. However, we have treated, and must continue to treat, of Antimony alone.

Now, no man has cause to fear that this Oil of Antimony—extracted first with distilled vinegar, and afterward with the most pure and subtle Spirit of Wine, and then further exalted as highly as possible by fire <note>original: Vulcan</note>—will in any way purge the bowels, excite frequent stools, or cause any such bodily disturbances. It effects none of these things; instead, through sweat, urine, and saliva <note>original: spittle</note>, it expels the very root of the disease to a degree that causes amazement, and restores whatever has been corrupted by any symptom.

But common Glass of Antimony <note>A translucent, reddish-brown glass made by roasting antimony ore; used as a potent medicine</note>, when ground to powder and put into a little wine (specifically six grains or more of the glass, according to the strength of the patient's constitution), and that mixture set in heat for one night, and in the morning the wine <margin>This is a common vomitory, or emetic wine, well known, which all wandering empirics <note>Unlicensed or fraudulent doctors</note> now use in all places, sometimes with prosperous and sometimes with the contrary, always ambiguous success. This proceeds partly from the profession and partly from the medicine. This is a thing to be bewailed.</margin> poured off clear from the remaining powder and so drunk by the patient, purges downward by exciting several stools. Oftentimes it also provokes vomiting because of the immature "mercurial" property which is still inherent in the Glass, as every intelligent physician will easily judge. Indeed, the physician ought further to examine how this Glass, when he intends to purge with it, should be tested and administered in a proper dose.

As histories relate, George Castriot <note>Skanderbeg (1405–1468), a nobleman who led a rebellion against the Ottoman Empire</note>, King of the Epirotes, was once asked by the Emperor of the Turks how the sword he had sent—following their peace agreement—could perform such wonders as were spoken of. The Emperor said he saw nothing singular in the weapon. Castriot gave this answer: *"I did indeed send the sword of Skanderbeg, but not Skanderbeg's arm, with which that sword was managed so as to perform so great..."*

<summary>The author distinguishes between the refined Oil of Antimony, which heals gently through sweat and urine, and the harsher Glass of Antimony, which acts as a violent emetic. He warns that the success of such potent medicines depends as much on the skill of the physician as on the substance itself.</summary>

### Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

Miracles so many chemical medicines are either dead, or (which is more to be deplored) oftentimes the causes of death when not managed by the hand of a skillful doctor. This unhappy failure of the medicine is caused by the mercurial properties with which it is too much impregnated (as Basilius well notes in this place) and which I am accustomed to correct in this manner:

Take <term>Glass of Antimony</term> <note>original: "Glafs of Antimony"; a translucent orange or brownish glass-like substance made by roasting antimony ore</note>, most pure, 4 ounces. Take <term>Venetian Borax</term> <note>a high-quality flux used in alchemy and glassmaking</note> half an ounce. Melt them together. This being skillfully done, you will have a green glass as transparent as an <term>emerald</term>. Grind this to a fine powder, upon which pour French wine, and permit the mixture to stand for several days in a moderate heat.

Of this wine give to the sick from 1 to 3 ounces, according to their age, habit of body, strength, and other things—either natural or not natural—which the prudence of the physician (when he sits as judge over whether the life of any person is likely to be continued or not) ought always to consider well. Experience, the most certain mistress of physicians, has taught me that this <term>emetic wine</term> <note>original: "Emerick Wine"; a wine infused with antimony used to induce vomiting</note> may be given to the sick without peril. And yet there is more than this: for when the wine poured onto the powder is all used up, if you pour on more French wine, that will also be imbued with the same virtues. For here <term>antimony</term> reveals itself to be something that is of all wonderful things the most admirable: because it contains within itself inexhaustible treasures, and although you take from it, you do not diminish its virtues. A similarly stupendous <term>miracle</term> is found in extracting the <term>vinegar</term> of the same, and in other works fit to be kept under the seal of <term>Harpocrates</term> <note>the Greek god of silence and secrets; implies the knowledge should be kept secret</note>.

Many men are required for searching out the powers and virtues of this subject. Because of the shortness of my time, I alone could not dive into and search out all things. Therefore, set about the work yourself, and after me—indeed, with me—you will praise me and I you. If you find out anything more, I praise you through these my writings, and shall commend you from the grave to which I am destined, although in body you are unknown to me, nor did I ever have any conversation with you, because perhaps you were not yet born.

Common <term>Glass of Antimony</term> is also, by addition, distilled into a praiseworthy and healthy oil, which may be used without danger and with very great profit in treating <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "Epilepsie"</note>, as I shall teach in the following section.

Grind the <term>Glass of Antimony</term> to as fine a powder as you possibly can, then put it into a glass vessel with a flat bottom, and pour upon the powder...

<summary>The author provides a recipe for "emetic wine" using Glass of Antimony and Borax, claiming the mineral's medicinal virtues are inexhaustible even after repeated use. He encourages future generations of physicians to continue his research into the hidden properties of Antimony.</summary>
<keywords>Basil Valentine, Antimony, Glass of Antimony, emetic wine, alchemy, pharmacology, epilepsy, Harpocrates</keywords>

# Chariot of Antimony.

Pour the juice of unripe grapes over it, then having well sealed <note>original: "luted"</note> the vessel, let it digest for a certain number of days. When this is finished, draw off all the juice. Afterward, grind the substance well while moistening it with spirit of vinegar and double its weight of clarified sugar. Then, having put it into a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note>, in the name of the Most High begin to distill. At the last stage, apply a vehement fire, and you will obtain a deep red oil; this must be clarified until it is transparent using spirit of wine.

<margin>When the author says, *it must be clarified with spirit of wine unto transparency*, the instruction is short indeed, but of great weight. For he intends that this oil should be driven over by an alembic <note>a distilling apparatus</note>; the meaning and manual operation of which I have already taught above.</margin>

The use of this oil, given in a small quantity, is found to be most profitable. Spirit of salt <note>hydrochloric acid</note> may be joined with this oil, and the mixture poured upon a finely prepared powder <note>original: "calx"</note> of gold (the making of which I have already taught in others of my writings). This mixture, together with its solvent <note>original: "menstruum"</note>, takes to itself the essence <note>original: "tincture"</note> of the gold only, and leaves the physical body of the metal untouched.

<margin>Do you think, O lover of chemistry, that you understand what you read? You cannot understand unless either a divine Philip <note>a reference to the biblical Philip explaining scripture to the Ethiopian eunuch</note> or a human Oedipus <note>the Greek mythological figure famous for solving the riddle of the Sphinx</note> appears to you and clearly teaches you the way of preparing this tincture. The difficulty of the riddle <note>original: "enigma"</note> consists in this: namely, that all solvents with which tinctures are extracted must necessarily be colorless. Otherwise, how can you know whether you have obtained the tincture you wished to extract, or have only recovered the same colored liquid you poured on? I will not detain you with a tedious discourse full of ambiguities, but will lead you as if by the hand, showing you how I established this process in the year 1665. If you reap any profit from this by your attention—and by comparing the preceding steps with the present, and these with those that follow—open the bosom of your heart so that no part of this knowledge may fall to the earth.</margin>

I took that red oil, prepared as far as has already been shown, and rectified it by a retort; I then acquired a white oil of an acidic but pleasant taste.

<summary>This page describes the distillation of a "Red Oil" from antimony using grape juice and sugar, and explains how this oil can be used to extract the "tincture" or essence of gold. The commentator emphasizes the difficulty of the process, noting that the solvent must be made colorless to prove the extraction was successful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical instructions of Basil Valentine, focusing on the preparation of a medicinal tincture derived from gold and the importance of choosing the correct solvents.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>Basil Valentine's Triumphant Chariot of Antimony</header>

Upon this I poured half as much spirit of salt <note>original: "Spirit of Salt"; modern: hydrochloric acid</note>. I digested the mixture in a vial for a full month so that these two spirits might be well conjoined. Afterward, for their better conjunction, I distilled them three times by retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling</note>.

Then I poured them upon the calx of gold <note>original: "Calx of Gold"; gold reduced to a fine powder through heat or chemical action</note>—the method of preparing which you will find in other writings of Basilius—and set them together in digestion for a month. When that time had elapsed, the menstruum <note>original: "Menstruum"; a solvent used to extract a substance</note> was tinged with a deep yellow color inclining toward redness. I leisurely poured off the tincture, and having put it into a retort, I drew off the moisture with a gentle fire so that a red powder might remain at the bottom. 

I edulcorated <note>original: "edulcorated"; washed or purified of acid and salts</note> this powder with distilled water and again extracted the tincture with spirit of wine <note>original: "Spirit of Wine"; ethanol or high-proof alcohol</note>. Then I rendered this "dragon" volatile and gave him his own tail to be devoured <note>an alchemical metaphor for a circular distillation process where the substance acts upon itself</note> for six whole months. I obtained a tincture most pleasant and grateful; ten or twelve grains of this, given to the sick, provoke sweat, comfort the natural powers, and—not to bore the intelligent with many words—it is an universal medicine for all diseases of both human and other bodies.

Since I have happened to mention the most excellent of all tinctures here, I will teach the chemist what will be of use to him for the future. That is to say, it is of great concern to know with what solvent every tincture should be extracted. For it is not sufficient—according to the erroneous opinion of many—that solvents be sweet and free of corrosion. It is also necessary that there be in them a peculiar affinity and suitability with the "mercury of the body" <note>the essential fluid or metallic principle</note> upon which they are poured, so that they may extract its true and sincere sulphur <note>the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance</note> from the same. 

By way of example, I shall teach you somewhat more clearly. Distilled water extracts the tincture from sulphur made of the glass of antimony <note>a transparent, reddish-brown glass produced by roasting antimony</note> by distilled vinegar. But if you persuade yourself that you have separated the pure from the impure in this operation, you will be deceived; for this water absorbs a certain salt which infects the tincture. Instead, rectified spirit of wine is its proper solvent, because that alone takes up the volatile sulphur and has no interaction with the salt.

The same happens in the tincture of corals, which is extracted with spirit of wine distilled upon orange peels. Here you do not obtain the sincere tincture of corals because, by this solvent, the pure sulphur is not separated from the body of the corals. I have observed by experience—which should be esteemed the best mistress—that similar errors are committed in many other things, unless one takes for granted that expensive and fruitless processes are necessarily required in this art. 

But to conclude with the tincture from which all this discourse proceeded: I would have no man think this to be the *Aurum potabile* <note>Latin: "potable gold," a legendary alchemical life-extender</note> of the philosophers. That would be a very great error in philosophy and give occasion to sophisters <note>those who use clever but false arguments</note>, as is their manner, to prefer their fallacies over the truth itself. Although this tincture is most precious and is a tincture of gold, it has only assumed the color of gold; the weight of it, which is proper to *Aurum potabile*, does not adhere to it.

Regarding the potable medicine, this is not the place for speaking, unless I would transgress the limits I have prescribed for myself at this time in this commentary...

<summary>The author describes a month-long process for creating a medicinal gold tincture and emphasizes the importance of matching specific solvents to substances to ensure purity. He warns that while this gold-colored medicine is powerful, it should not be confused with the true "Potable Gold" of the philosophers.</summary>

### The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony

I am reflecting upon the *Triumphant Chariot of Antimony* <note>original: "Chariot of Antimony"; a famous alchemical text attributed to Basil Valentine</note>, which our Basil has made so truly magnificent. Otherwise, I would explain how Gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> might be prepared by Copper <note>original: "Venus"</note> and Fire <note>original: "Vulcan"</note>, so that in the space of two hours it resolves itself into Quicksilver <note>original: "Mercury"</note> united with Wine <note>original: "Bacchus"</note>, leaving very little dross behind. This <term>Mercurial Menstruum</term> <note>A solvent derived from mercury used to dissolve metals</note> may again be separated from the dissolved Gold; in this way, you might acquire a most pleasing and very heavy liquid, which can never afterward be returned to its former solid state. I have shown this very operation to some curious lovers of chemistry. However, another occasion for writing about these things will be provided elsewhere.

When the fermentation is complete, I would need a vast quantity of paper to declare all the <term>Arcanums</term> <note>Secret or hidden mysteries of nature</note> of Nature which are achieved by this medicine, beyond what any man could imagine. I urge this so much the more earnestly to the physician, so that he may consider the things I propose philosophically, commit himself to the work, perform this preparation of antimony, and put it to use. Then, he will daily find more praise for himself and learn more operations from it than any other physician could have prescribed for him.

When you have brought antimony this far and duly perfected your work—in which you must act prudently, weighing the matter deeply and thoroughly so that you may acquire experience through labor—then you may boast that you have obtained the <term>Magistery</term> <note>A master-work or a sovereign medicinal preparation</note>, which is known or shared with very few. This Magistery, when mixed with a solution or tincture of corals and administered with a <term>Cordial Water</term> <note>A medicinal liquid intended to stimulate the heart and spirits</note>, effects wonders in diseases that must be cured by purifying the blood. Whatever ailment is presented to you in which the blood is corrupted by any accident, this Magistery heals it. It cheers the heart, promotes chastity and honesty, and renders a man capable and fit for everything he undertakes.

For all these benefits, thanks must always be given from the bottom of our hearts to the Creator and Preserver of all things; because he has...

<summary>The author describes a method for dissolving gold into a permanent liquid state and emphasizes the profound medicinal power of antimonial preparations for purifying the blood and improving character.</summary>

...with so great compassion respected his *creatures*, who are weak both in body and mind, and supplied us with *means* by which the *diseases* of either may be healed, and we may obtain solace, assistance, and perfect help in every necessity.

### The Construction and Use of the Distilling Apparatus

<image-desc>An engraving of a distillation apparatus. On the right, a cylindrical furnace (labeled A) with two square openings supports a retort (labeled B) with a rounded body and a curved neck. The neck of retort B connects to the side of a receiver vessel (labeled C). Vessel C rests on another cylindrical furnace (labeled F), also with two square openings. Above vessel C, a connecting piece (labeled D) leads to a larger, bulbous vessel (labeled E). Vessel E is topped with an alembic head from which a long, angled tube extends upwards and to the left.</image-desc>

The illustration above demonstrates the proper arrangement of the vessels for a complex distillation. 

**A** represents the first furnace, which provides the primary heat source for the initial separation. 

**B** is the **retort** <note>original: "retort"; a glass or metal vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</note>, which contains the raw material to be processed. As the heat from furnace **A** rises, the volatile vapors are driven out of the retort and into the next stage of the system.

**C** identifies the **receiver** <note>original: "receiuer"; a vessel designed to collect the condensed vapors from the retort</note>. In this specific configuration, the receiver sits atop a second furnace, marked **F**. This allows the chemist to apply a secondary, gentler heat to the collected liquid, further refining it or preventing it from solidifying too early.

**D** is the connecting pipe or "neck" that joins the receiver to the upper chamber.

**E** represents the large, bulbous vessel topped with an **alembic** head <note>original: "alembick"; a cap used in distilling that cools vapors so they condense and trickle down into a collection tube</note>. This part of the apparatus ensures that only the most refined and "subtle" spirits reach the final collection point, as the heavier elements fall back down into vessel **C**.

<margin>Note the importance of the seals at every joint to prevent the escape of precious spirits.</margin>

By using this multi-stage method, the practitioner can achieve a much higher degree of purity than a single distillation would allow. The heat must be carefully regulated in both furnace **A** and furnace **F** to ensure the vapors move steadily through the system without the glass breaking from excessive pressure.

<summary>This page provides a detailed diagram and explanation of a multi-stage distillation apparatus involving two furnaces, a retort, and an alembic. It explains how secondary heating in the receiver allows for the further refinement of chemical spirits.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, retort, receiver, furnace, alembic, chemistry, laboratory equipment, refinement</keywords>

<margin>The Furnace and Vessel</margin>

**A.** The lower part of the furnace, designed to contain the charcoal or fuel.
**B.** The dome or "reverberating" cover <note>A cover designed to reflect heat back down onto the vessel</note>, which concentrates the heat.
**C.** The **alembic** <term>alembic: a classic distillation vessel consisting of a boiler and a cap with a tube</term> or **retort** <term>retort: a glass bulb with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term> positioned to receive the rising vapors.

-> **The Method of Connection** <-

The pipe extending from the furnace to the secondary structure allows for the regulated flow of heat or vapor, ensuring that the substance in vessel **C** is heated evenly. This setup is essential for the extraction of essential oils and the purification of chemical spirits.

<page-num>82</page-num>

### The Description of the Distillation Apparatus

The engraving above illustrates a sophisticated arrangement for the distillation and refinement of chemical substances.

->**The Components of the Machine**<-

**A** represents the first furnace, which provides the heat source for the initial stage of the process.
**B** is a round-bottomed glass vessel <note>original: "alembic"</note> or flask, which contains the raw material to be heated.
**C** is the secondary receiving vessel, positioned upon its own furnace or stand, marked **F**.
**D** serves as the connecting neck or joint that bridges the lower vessel to the upper chamber.
**E** is the large, pear-shaped condensing chamber where the vapors are collected and cooled.
The cap at the very top, equipped with a side arm, allows for the collection of the final refined spirit or the escape of air to prevent the vessels from bursting under pressure.

<margin>The use of two furnaces allows for precise temperature control.</margin>

This specific configuration is designed so that the heat from furnace **A** drives the vapors from vessel **B** into the central assembly. By placing vessel **C** on its own stand **F**, the chemist can apply a secondary heat source if the process requires the substance to remain in a gaseous state before reaching the final condenser **E**. 

<note>This setup is a precursor to modern fractional distillation, where different components of a mixture are separated based on their boiling points.</note>

As the ancient philosophers said, *Nature performs its operations by degrees* <note>original: "Natura operatur gradatim"</note>. Therefore, the practitioner must ensure that the joints are sealed tightly with "philosopher's luting" <term>luting</term> <note>A clay or paste used to seal chemical apparatus to make them airtight.</note> so that the subtle spirits do not escape into the air.

<summary>This page provides a detailed diagram and explanation of a dual-furnace distillation apparatus used for refining chemical spirits. It identifies the individual components—furnaces, flasks, and condensers—and explains their functional relationship in the laboratory.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, alembic, furnace, chemistry, laboratory apparatus, luting, vapors, refinement</keywords>
