## The Practice.

My son, it is necessary that you work with the *Mercury* of the *Philosophers* and of the *wise*. This is not the *common* <note>original: "Vulgar"; referring to ordinary, commercially available substances rather than the refined philosophical versions</note> mercury, nor does it have any of the qualities of the *common* kind. Instead, according to the masters, it is the *First Matter*, the *Soul of the World*, the *Cold Element*, the *Blessed Water*, the *Water of the Wise*, the *Venomous Water*, the *Most Sharp Vinegar*, the *Mineral Water*, the *Water of Celestial Grace*, the *Virgin's Milk*, and our *Mineral and Corporeal Mercury*.

For this substance alone perfects both of the stones: the *White* and the *Red*. Consider what *Geber* <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century polymath and "father of chemistry"</note> says: that our *Art* does not consist of a multitude of different things, because this *Mercury* is only one single thing. That is to say, it is the one and only *Stone* in which the entire *Magistery* <term>the masterwork or the full secret process of alchemy</term> consists. You shall not add any foreign thing to it, except that during its preparation, you shall remove from it whatever is unnecessary. This is because all things required for this *Art* are contained within this single matter.

Therefore, it is very important to note that he says we must add nothing foreign, except for the *Sun* and *Moon* for the red and white *Tincture* <term>a medicine or elixir used to transform base metals into gold or silver</term>. These are not "foreign" to the substance, but are its *Ferment* <note>original: "Ferment"; in alchemy, this acts like yeast, "leavening" the base matter to raise it to a higher state</note>, by which the work is accomplished.

Lastly, observe, my son, that these *Suns* and *Moons* are not the same as *common gold* and *silver*. Our *Suns* and *Moons* are superior in their nature to the *common* ones. Our *Suns* and *Moons* are "living" by their very nature, while those of the common people are "dead" in comparison to ours, which exist and remain permanently within our *Stone*. From this, you may observe that the *Mercury* drawn out of our bodies <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to the metallic substances being used in the experiment</note> is like the *watery and common mercury*. For that reason, it enjoys itself and takes pleasure in its own kind, and is more glad of...

<summary>This page defines "Philosophical Mercury" as the singular, essential substance of alchemy, distinguishing it from common quicksilver and explaining how "living" gold and silver act as ferments to perfect the Stone.</summary>

This fact has not been revealed by the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists who studied the secrets of nature</note> in their books. Therefore, the advantage found in this Art lies in the *Mercury*, the *Sun* <note>Alchemical symbol for Gold</note>, and the *Moon* <note>Alchemical symbol for Silver</note>. Diomedes <note>An authority often cited in alchemical texts, representing the ancient tradition of "the wise"</note> says: make use of a matter to which you must not introduce any foreign thing, neither powder nor water. This is because adding various things does not improve our *Stone*. By this, he sufficiently instructs anyone who understands him that the tincture <term>the transformative essence or elixir</term> of our *Stone* is drawn from nothing but the *Mercury of the Philosophers*. This is their principle, their root, and their great Tree, sprouting forth into boughs and branches.

## The first Operation,

# SUBLIMATION.

This is not the common <note>original: "Vulgar"</note> process, but the philosophical one. Through it, we remove from the *Stone* whatever is unnecessary. In effect, this is nothing else but the rising up of the volatile <term>original: "not-fixed"; parts of a substance that vaporize or "fly" when heated</term> part through smoke and vapor. The fixed <term>the stable part of a substance that resists the heat of the fire and does not evaporate</term> part should remain at the bottom. We do not intend for one to be separated from the other, but rather that they remain and become fixed together. 

Know also that whoever sublimes our *Philosophical Mercury* (which contains all the power of our *Stone*) exactly as it should be done, shall perfect the Magistery <term>the complete masterwork or the secret of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term>. This led Geber <note>A Latinized name for Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy</note> to say that all perfection consists in *Sublimation*. Within this *Sublimation* are all other operations—that is to say, Distillation,

...tion <note>The text likely completes the word "conjunction" or "sublimation" from the previous page.</note>, <term>Assation</term> <note>original: "Assation"; the process of roasting or drying a substance using heat</note>, <term>Destruction</term>, <term>Coagulation</term> <note>the process of a liquid thickening into a solid</note>, <term>Putrefaction</term> <note>the stage of rotting or breaking down matter</note>, <term>Calcination</term> <note>reducing a substance to a powder through high heat</note>, <term>Fixation</term> <note>making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated</note>, and <term>Reduction</term> of the <term>White and Red Tinctures</term> <note>the "tinctures" are the transformative essences: the White produces silver and the Red produces gold</note>. These are created and produced in one furnace and in one vessel, and this is the direct path to the final completion, about which the Philosophers <note>alchemists</note> have written various chapters specifically to confuse <note>original: "amuse"</note> the ignorant.

Take then, in the name of the great God, the respected material of the Philosophers, called the primary matter <note>original: "Hyle"; a Greek term for the fundamental substance of the universe</note> of the wise, which contains the previously mentioned <term>Philosophical Mercury</term>. This is called the first matter of the perfect body. Put it into its vessel—which must be clear, transparent, and round—and close it tightly with the <term>Seal of Seals</term> <note>a hermetic or airtight seal</note>. Heat it in its properly prepared place with a moderate heat for the duration of a <term>Philosophical Month</term> <note>often considered a period of 40 days</note>, keeping it for six weeks and two days in the "sweat" of <term>Sublimation</term> until it begins to rot, to ferment, to take on color, and to congeal with its metallic moisture. It must be "fixed" to the point that it no longer rises as an airy, smoky substance, but remains settled at the bottom, transformed from what it was. It should be stripped of all sticky moisture, rotted and black; this state is called the "Black Robe," "Night," or the <term>Crow’s-head</term> <note>original: "Crowes-head" or "Caput Corvi"; the traditional name for the "Nigredo" or blackening stage, symbolizing the death of the old matter before its rebirth</note>. 

Thus, when our "Stone" is in the vessel and it rises up high in smoke, this is called <term>Sublimation</term>; when it falls back down from above, it is called <term>Distillation</term> and <term>Descension</term>. When it begins to take on the qualities of the smoky substance and to rot—and because of the frequent rising and falling, it begins to thicken—then it is <term>Putrefaction</term> and "consuming <term>Sulphur</term>." Lastly, through the lack or deprivation of the moisture of the "radical water," <term>Calcination</term> and <term>Fixation</term> are achieved at the same time, by heating <note>original: "decoction"</note> alone in only one vessel, as I have already said. Moreover, in this sublimation, the true separation of the Elements is achieved...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical process of sublimation, specifically describing the transition of the "Elixir" through its elemental stages and the significance of the "blackness" that appears during the work.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>Concerning the Philosophers' Stone.</header>
<meta>catchword: day,</meta>

For in our sublimation, the *Elixir* is turned from water into a dry and hot terrestrial element. By this operation, it is clear that the separation of the four elements in our Stone is not *vulgar* <note>original: "vulgar"; meaning common, literal, or the crude methods used by non-alchemists</note> but *philosophical*. Because of this, there are only two *formal* elements in our Stone: Earth and Water. However, the Earth contains within its physical density the power and dryness of Fire, and the Water contains within itself the Air and its moisture.

Thus, we visibly see only two elements in our Stone, but in effect, there are four. By this, you may judge that the separation of the four elements is entirely physical in a philosophical sense, rather than the literal and crude methods that the ignorant practice daily.

Continue the decoction <note>the process of heating or boiling a substance</note> with a gentle fire until all the black matter appearing on the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> is completely cleared away by the Magistery <note>the master process or the chemical operation itself</note>. Philosophers call this blackness the "dark mantle" of the Stone. After it becomes clear, it is termed the "cleansing water of the earth," or more accurately, the Elixir.

Note that the appearance of blackness is a sign of putrefaction <note>the breakdown or "rotting" of the base material, essential for its transformation</note>. The beginning of the dissolution is a sign of the conjunction <note>joining together</note> of both natures. This blackness sometimes appears in about forty days, more or less, depending on the quantity of the material and the skill of the operator, which contributes much to the removal of said blackness.

Now, my son, by the grace of God, you are acquainted with one element of our Stone: the "black earth," also known as the "Raven's Head" or the "obscure shadow." This earth serves as the base upon which all the rest is grounded. This terrestrial and dry element is called *Laton* <note>an alchemical term for the base metal or "lead" that must be purified</note>, *the Bull*, *black dregs*, *our metal*, and *our Mercury*. Thus, by removing the scorched <note>original: "adust"</note> moisture through philosophical sublimation, the volatile substance is fixed <note>made stable and solid</note>, and the moist is made dry and turned into earth.

No, according to *Geber* <note>original: "Geber"; refers to Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath and a foundational figure in alchemy</note>, a change in temperament <note>original: "complexion"; in this context, the balance of internal qualities</note> is brought about—converting a cold and humid nature into dry yellow bile <note>original: "choler"</note>; and according to *Alphidius* <note>an early Arabic alchemical author frequently cited in medieval texts</note>, it changes from a liquid into a solid <note>original: "thick"</note>. From this, we understand what the philosophers mean when they say that the operation of our Stone is only a transmutation of nature and a revolution of the elements. You see, then, how through this incorporation the moist becomes dry, the volatile becomes fixed <note>stable under heat</note>, the spiritual becomes physical <note>original: "corporal"</note>, the liquid becomes solid, water becomes fire, and air becomes earth. There occurs an absolute change in their true nature and a specific circulation of the four elements.

## The Second Operation,

# DEALBATION.
<note>original: "DEALBATION"; the process of whitening or bleaching the alchemical matter</note>

It converts our *mercury* into the *white stone*, and does so through heating <note>original: "decoction"</note> alone. When the earth is separated from its water, the vessel must be placed on the ashes—as is common in a distilling furnace—and the water must be distilled with a gentle fire at the start, so that the water drips so slowly that you could distinctly count as many as *forty* names or pronounce fifty-six words between drops. 

Let this procedure be followed in all distillations of the black earth. The matter that remains at the bottom of the vessel—the *dregs* <note>original: "faces" or "faeces"; the solid sediment left after distillation</note>—must be absorbed <note>original: "imbibed"</note> by the new water and will eventually be dissolved. This water should be three or four parts greater in volume than those *dregs*, so that everything may be dissolved and converted into mercury and *quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver"</note>. I tell you that this must be done so often that

...nothing will remain but the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "Murc"; refers to the dregs or solid pulp remaining after distillation</note>. There is no set time limit for this distillation; rather, it occurs sooner or later depending on whether there is a greater or lesser quantity of water in proportion to the intensity of the fire. 

Then take the solid material (the "earth") that you have kept in a glass vessel, along with its distilled water. Using a soft and gentle fire—similar to the one used for distillation or purification, or perhaps slightly stronger—continue the process until the earth becomes dry and white, having absorbed all its water because of its dryness. 

Once this is done, add some of the previously mentioned water to it and, just as you did at the beginning, continue the same <term>heating</term> <note>original: "decoction"; the process of boiling or heating a substance in a liquid</note> until that earth becomes absolutely white, purified, and clear, and has absorbed all its water. Note that this earth will be washed of its blackness by the heating process, as I have said, because it is easily decomposed by its own water and thus cleansed. This is the completion of the <term>Magistery</term> <note>original: "Magistery"; the "Masterwork" or the successful completion of the alchemical process</note>; be sure to store that white earth very carefully. For that is the *White <term>Mercury</term>, White <term>Magnesia</term>*, and "Foliated Earth" <note>original: "Foliated earth"; refers to the earth once it has reached a layered or flaky state during the whitening process</note>.

Next, take this purified white earth and place it in its vessel over the ashes for a fire of <term>Sublimation</term> <note>the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the substance</note>. Apply a very strong heat until all the condensed water within rises into the <term>alembic</term> <note>an alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> and the earth remains at the bottom thoroughly <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to a dry powder or purified state through high heat</note>. 

Now you have the earth, the water, and the air. Although the earth possesses the nature of fire, it is not yet visible in its effects; you will see this when, through further heating, you cause it to turn red. At that stage, you will clearly see the appearance of the fire. Such must be the procedure for the <term>Fermentation</term> <note>in alchemy, the process of adding a "soul" or active power to a "dead" substance to multiply its strength</note> of the white earth, so that the "dead body" [the solid matter] may be animated and brought to life, and its power increased infinitely. Note, however, that the <term>ferment</term> cannot enter the dead body except by means of the water,

It concludes by preparing this "white earth" for the subsequent stage of reddening and fermentation, which gives the stone its active power.</summary>

...which has made a contract and a marriage between the <term>Ferment</term> <note>original: "Ferment"; a substance used to trigger a transformation or "leavening" in the alchemical matter</note> and the white earth. Know that in all fermentation, the weight must be carefully considered so that the quantity of the <term>volatile</term> <note>original: "volatile"; a substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated</note> does not exceed the <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "fixed"; a substance that is stable and resistant to heat</note>, causing the "marriage" to vanish in smoke. 

For, as *Senior* <note>original: "Senior"; likely referring to Senior Zadith (Zadith ben Hamuel), a famous 10th-century alchemist often quoted in Western texts</note> says, if you do not convert the earth into water, and the water into fire, there cannot be a union of the spirit and the body. To achieve this, take a <term>lamen</term> <note>original: "Lamen"; a thin plate of metal</note> or red-hot plate and cast a drop of our <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: "Medicine"; the alchemical substance in its transformative state</note> onto it. If it penetrates the metal, it will turn a perfect color, which is a sign of perfection. If it does not color the metal, repeat the process of <term>dissolution</term> <note>dissolving the solid into liquid</note> and <term>coagulation</term> <note>thickening the liquid back into a solid</note> until it does color and penetrate. 

Note that seven <term>imbibitions</term> <note>original: "imbibitions"; the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</note> at most are sufficient, and five at the least, so that the matter may be liquefied without smoke. At that point, the matter is perfect in its whiteness. This process sometimes requires a longer time to be fixed and sometimes a shorter time, depending on the quantity of the Medicine used. Note also that our Medicine requires seven months from the creation of our Mercury to achieve whiteness, and five more to arrive at redness—which, when added together, make twelve months in total.

Take as much of the white Medicine as you wish and place it in its glass vessel upon the hot ashes until it becomes as dry as the ashes themselves. Then add some <term>water of the Sun</term> <note>original: "water of the Sun"; likely referring to "gold water" or a solar-infused solvent used for the reddening stage</note> which you have...

...kept purposely for that end. Continue the fire at the second degree <note>original: "second degree"; a moderate level of heat in alchemical practice, often compared to the heat of the sun in midsummer</note> until the matter becomes dry. Then, add some of the above-mentioned water again, and successively soak <note>original: "imbibe"</note> and dry it until the matter is turned red <note>original: "rubified"</note> and becomes as fluid as wax. Cover the red-hot plate <note>original: "red Lamen"</note> with it, as has been described, and the matter shall be perfect in its **redness**. 

But note that each time, you need to add no more of the "water of the Sun" <note>original: "water of the Sun"; likely a reference to a gold-based solution or a specific acidic solvent used in the reddening stage</note> than is strictly necessary to cover the body of the matter. This is done so that the Elixir does not sink and become "drowned." The fire must be continued until the matter is completely dried <note>original: "desiccation"</note>, and then a second soaking <note>original: "imbibition"</note> must be performed. Proceed in this order until the medicine is perfected—that is to say, until the force of the fire's digestion converts it into a very red powder. This is the true matter <note>original: "Hyle"; from the Greek word *hyle* (ὕλη) meaning "matter" or "substance"</note> of the philosophers: the "bloody stone," the "purple-red coral," the "precious ruby," "red mercury," and the "red tincture."

# PROJECTION <note>original: "Projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process where the Stone is cast upon base metals to transmute them into gold or silver</note>

The more often you dissolve and solidify <note>original: "coagulate"</note> it, the more its power will be multiplied infinitely. However, note that the medicine is multiplied more slowly by *Solution* than by *Fermentation*. Therefore, a dissolved substance does not work well if it is not first fixed in its ferment. Nevertheless, the multiplication of the medicine by solution is more abundant than that of the ferment, because there is a greater refining of the substance <note>original: "subtilization"</note>. Yet, I advise you that during this multiplication, you should place one part of the finished work upon four parts of the other substance, and in a short time, a powder will be created that is entirely made of ferment,

<meta>This page concludes the treatise on the Philosopher’s Stone by providing an interpretive epilogue attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, explaining the allegorical language used in the "Emerald Tablet," a foundational text of alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>The true Book</header>

## The Epilogue according to
### HERMES.

In this way, you are to separate the *earth from the fire* and the *gross from the subtle* gently and with great judgment. That is to say, separate the parts that are united in the furnace through the dissolution and separation of the components—such as the *earth from the fire* and the *subtle from the gross*, and so on. This means extracting the purer substance of the Stone until you have made it clean and free from all impurities <note>original: "spots or filth"</note>.

And when he says, "*it ascends from the earth up into heaven and returns again to the earth*," this refers simply to the Sublimation <note>original: "Sublimation of the Bodies"; the alchemical process where a solid substance is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into solid form, often symbolizing purification</note> of the substances. 

Furthermore, to explain what distillation is, he says "*the wind carries it in its belly*." This occurs when the liquid is distilled by the Alembic <note>original: "Alembick"; an early alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note>, where it first rises as a "wind" full of fumes and vapor, and afterwards returns to the bottom of the vessel as a liquid once more. 

When he also wishes to express the solidifying <note>original: "congelation"</note> of the matter, he says, "*Its force is absolute, if it be turned into earth*." That is to say, it is transformed by boiling <note>original: "decoction"; the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or change its state</note>. 

And to provide a general summary of everything that has been said, he says, "*It shall receive both the inferior and superior force*." This refers to the power of the Elements. For if the Medicine <note>original: "Medicine"; here meaning the Philosopher's Stone or the transformative elixir</note> receives the force of the lighter parts (namely, air and fire), it shall also receive that of the heavier and weightier parts (transforming itself into water and earth). The goal is for these substances to be perpetually joined together so they may achieve permanence, endurance, constancy, and stability. Glory be to God.

->THE END <note>original: "FINIS"</note><-

<summary>This page provides a concluding interpretation of the Hermetic philosophy, decoding the metaphorical language of the Emerald Tablet into practical alchemical operations like distillation, sublimation, and decoction. It emphasizes the union of "superior" and "inferior" elemental forces to achieve a stable and perfect "Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
